### Learning to Generate Structured Output with Schema Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18878v1)
- **Authors**: Yaxi Lu, Haolun Li, Xin Cong, Zhong Zhang, Yesai Wu, Yankai Lin, Zhiyuan Liu, Fangming Liu, Maosong Sun
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the structured generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs), focusing on producing valid JSON outputs against a given schema. Despite the widespread use of JSON in integrating language models with programs, there is a lack of comprehensive analysis and benchmarking of these capabilities. We explore various aspects of JSON generation, such as structure understanding, escaping, and natural language description, to determine how to assess and enable LLMs to generate valid responses. Building upon this, we propose SchemaBench features around 40K different JSON schemas to obtain and assess models' abilities in generating valid JSON. We find that the latest LLMs are still struggling to generate a valid JSON string. Moreover, we demonstrate that incorporating reinforcement learning with a Fine-grained Schema Validator can further enhance models' understanding of JSON schema, leading to improved performance. Our models demonstrate significant improvement in both generating JSON outputs and downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SchemaBench, a benchmark for evaluating large language models' (LLMs) ability to generate valid JSON outputs according to given schemas.  It identifies challenges in structure understanding, escaping, and following natural language descriptions within schemas. The paper proposes Schema Reinforcement Learning (SRL), a training method incorporating a fine-grained schema validator and "Thoughts of Structure" (ToS) to improve JSON generation. The authors demonstrate that SRL enhances model performance on both JSON generation and downstream tasks (BFCL), exceeding supervised fine-tuning baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: enabling LLMs to reliably generate structured outputs, particularly JSON. While JSON is widely used, existing methods and benchmarks have limitations in dealing with complex schemas and nuanced constraints.

**Novelty:**

*   **Benchmark (SchemaBench):** The creation of a dataset of approximately 40,000 JSON schemas is a significant contribution. The paper presents a detailed description of the data collection and cleaning pipeline. This benchmark has the potential to serve as a valuable resource for the community, enabling more rigorous evaluation of LLMs' structured output generation capabilities.
*   **Schema Reinforcement Learning (SRL):** The use of reinforcement learning with a fine-grained schema validator is a novel approach. The idea of incorporating RL to address the sensitivity of JSON formatting and optimize for nuanced correctness is well-reasoned.  The combination of SRL with Thoughts of Structure (ToS) is a further innovation.
*   **Thoughts of Structure (ToS):** While inspired by Chain-of-Thought prompting, ToS adapts the reasoning process specifically to the generation of JSON structures. Training the model to explicitly outline the reasoning steps before producing JSON could improve its ability to handle complex schemas and dependencies.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved JSON Generation:** The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in JSON generation accuracy using SRL, particularly when compared to supervised fine-tuning. The gains on complex schemas are especially noteworthy.
*   **Downstream Task Performance:** The positive results on BFCL indicate that the improvements in JSON generation translate to tangible benefits in downstream applications. This is a crucial finding, showing that the research is not simply optimizing for a specific metric but rather improving the usability of LLMs in real-world scenarios.
*   **Practical Implications:** Reliable JSON generation is essential for integrating LLMs with existing systems and APIs. By improving this capability, the paper makes LLMs more versatile and useful in a wider range of applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Analysis of ToS:** While ToS is presented as a key component, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of its effectiveness. Are there specific types of schemas or tasks where ToS is most beneficial?
*   **Benchmark Diversity:** While the 40K schema dataset is substantial, further information on the distribution of schema types and complexities could be provided. Are certain types of schemas over- or under-represented?
*   **Generalization:** While the paper shows improvements on BFCL, additional experiments on other downstream tasks would strengthen the claims about generalization.

**Justification:**

The paper is well-motivated, presents a clearly defined problem, and proposes a novel solution. The benchmark, SRL framework, and ToS component represent valuable contributions to the field. While some areas could benefit from further analysis, the paper demonstrates a significant advancement in enabling LLMs to generate valid and useful JSON outputs. The significant performance gains and the potential impact on downstream tasks justify a relatively high score.

### From Hours to Minutes: Lossless Acceleration of Ultra Long Sequence Generation up to 100K Tokens
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18890v1)
- **Authors**: Tong Wu, Junzhe Shen, Zixia Jia, Yuxuan Wang, Zilong Zheng
- **Abstract**: Generating ultra-long sequences with large language models (LLMs) has become increasingly crucial but remains a highly time-intensive task, particularly for sequences up to 100K tokens. While traditional speculative decoding methods exist, simply extending their generation limits fails to accelerate the process and can be detrimental. Through an in-depth analysis, we identify three major challenges hindering efficient generation: frequent model reloading, dynamic key-value (KV) management and repetitive generation. To address these issues, we introduce TOKENSWIFT, a novel framework designed to substantially accelerate the generation process of ultra-long sequences while maintaining the target model's inherent quality. Experimental results demonstrate that TOKENSWIFT achieves over 3 times speedup across models of varying scales (1.5B, 7B, 8B, 14B) and architectures (MHA, GQA). This acceleration translates to hours of time savings for ultra-long sequence generation, establishing TOKENSWIFT as a scalable and effective solution at unprecedented lengths. Code can be found at https://github.com/bigai-nlco/TokenSwift.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TokenSwift, a novel framework designed to accelerate the generation of ultra-long sequences (up to 100K tokens) with Large Language Models (LLMs) without sacrificing output quality. The framework addresses three key challenges: frequent model reloading, dynamic Key-Value (KV) cache management, and repetitive content generation. TokenSwift utilizes n-gram retrieval and dynamic KV cache updates, alongside a contextual penalty, to achieve significant speedups (over 3x) compared to standard autoregressive generation across various model sizes and architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Incremental improvement on existing speculative decoding techniques:** While the paper builds upon existing work in speculative decoding (SD) and multi-token generation (e.g., Medusa, TriForce), it adapts and extends these methods to the challenging domain of *ultra*-long sequence generation (100K tokens). The adaptation is not trivial, as standard SD techniques struggle with such lengths due to KV cache limitations and the need for frequent model reloads.
    *   **Combination of Techniques:** The novelty primarily lies in the *combination* of specific strategies (n-gram retrieval, dynamic KV cache, and contextual penalty) and their optimization for this specific problem. Individually, these techniques have been explored in other contexts, but their synergistic application to ultra-long generation is the key contribution.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The work is primarily driven by practical considerations - the need for efficient generation of lengthy content. The theoretical innovation might be limited, focusing more on an engineering solution to a practical problem.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addressing a Practical Bottleneck:** Ultra-long sequence generation is crucial for various applications, including creative writing, reasoning, and agentic behavior. By significantly reducing generation time, TokenSwift makes these applications more feasible.
    *   **Model Agnostic:**  A strength of the paper is its claim of being model agnostic. The results seem to support this claim, showcasing improvements across diverse architectures (MHA, GQA) and model scales.
    *   **Scalability:** The approach is claimed to be scalable, but further investigation would be required to evaluate the framework's performance with even larger models or much larger datasets. While the paper demonstrated a successful transfer onto various architectures, this does not automatically imply scalability, which must be evaluated on a different setting.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results across various models, prefix lengths, and generation lengths. The ablation studies are particularly helpful in understanding the contribution of each component of the TokenSwift framework.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The challenges of ultra-long sequence generation are clearly articulated and well-motivated.
    *   **Lossless Accuracy:** The paper emphasizes the importance of maintaining the target model's inherent quality, which is a strong selling point.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper focuses more on the practical implementation and empirical evaluation, lacking significant theoretical analysis or formal guarantees.
    *   **Dependence on Existing Techniques:** The method heavily relies on speculative decoding methods, which may be susceptible to certain limitations.
    *   **Distinct-n Score limitations:** Although distinct-n is used to measure diversity and lower repetition, it could be limited. Repetition can be on a semantic level, rather than a word-for-word repetition. Using other, perhaps more comprehensive, methods could be beneficial to ensure diversity.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could inspire further research into optimized KV cache management and token reutilization techniques for long sequence generation.
    *   The framework's practical benefits could lead to its adoption in real-world applications.
    *   The analysis of challenges could guide the development of future algorithms specifically designed for the ultra-long sequence regime.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, TokenSwift provides a valuable practical solution to a significant bottleneck in LLM applications. The paper effectively adapts existing techniques and integrates them into a cohesive framework with strong empirical support. The novelty is primarily in the combination of methods and their optimization for the ultra-long sequence regime, rather than in fundamental algorithmic breakthroughs. The practical impact and potential for further research justify a good, but not outstanding score.

### An Empirical Study on Commit Message Generation using LLMs via In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18904v1)
- **Authors**: Yifan Wu, Yunpeng Wang, Ying Li, Wei Tao, Siyu Yu, Haowen Yang, Wei Jiang, Jianguo Li
- **Abstract**: Commit messages concisely describe code changes in natural language and are important for software maintenance. Several approaches have been proposed to automatically generate commit messages, but they still suffer from critical limitations, such as time-consuming training and poor generalization ability. To tackle these limitations, we propose to borrow the weapon of large language models (LLMs) and in-context learning (ICL). Our intuition is based on the fact that the training corpora of LLMs contain extensive code changes and their pairwise commit messages, which makes LLMs capture the knowledge about commits, while ICL can exploit the knowledge hidden in the LLMs and enable them to perform downstream tasks without model tuning. However, it remains unclear how well LLMs perform on commit message generation via ICL. In this paper, we conduct an empirical study to investigate the capability of LLMs to generate commit messages via ICL. Specifically, we first explore the impact of different settings on the performance of ICL-based commit message generation. We then compare ICL-based commit message generation with state-of-the-art approaches on a popular multilingual dataset and a new dataset we created to mitigate potential data leakage. The results show that ICL-based commit message generation significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches on subjective evaluation and achieves better generalization ability. We further analyze the root causes for LLM's underperformance and propose several implications, which shed light on future research directions for using LLMs to generate commit messages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) and In-Context Learning (ICL) for automatically generating commit messages from code changes. The authors explore the impact of different prompt settings and demonstration selection methods on the performance of ICL-based commit message generation. They compare various LLMs with state-of-the-art baselines on a popular benchmark dataset (MCMD) and a newly created dataset (MCMD-New) designed to mitigate potential data leakage. The evaluation includes objective metrics (BLEU, METEOR, ROUGE-L, Cider) and subjective metrics (human evaluation and LLM-based evaluation). The results indicate that ICL-based commit message generation can outperform state-of-the-art approaches, achieves better generalization ability, and is significantly influenced by prompt and demonstration settings.  Finally, the paper analyzes the causes of LLM underperformance in some cases and suggests directions for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this work is moderate.  While LLMs have been explored for various software engineering tasks, including code generation, the specific application to *commit message generation via in-context learning,* with an emphasis on a comprehensive empirical study including prompt engineering, demonstration selection, and a new dataset tailored for mitigating data leakage concerns, elevates it beyond a simple application. The detailed analysis of different prompt and demonstration settings, and the investigation of why LLMs sometimes fail, also adds to the novelty. The use of LLM-based evaluation metrics is a good step, and provides something new as human evaluation is expensive to conduct.

**Significance:** The paper contributes to the field of software engineering automation. Commit messages are crucial for software maintenance, and automating their generation can improve developer productivity and code quality. The finding that ICL with LLMs can achieve competitive or superior performance compared to fine-tuned models, while avoiding the resource-intensive training process, is significant. The insights into prompt engineering and demonstration selection provide practical guidance for applying LLMs to this task. The creation of a new dataset (MCMD-New) helps to address the critical concern of data leakage, which is a major issue in LLM research.  The root cause analysis, identifying issues like low-quality reference messages and lack of contextual knowledge, is also valuable for future research.  The confirmation that LLMs can be good evaluators provides value as an alternative to human annotation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Empirical Study:** The paper presents a thorough and well-designed empirical study, covering a wide range of LLMs, prompt settings, demonstration selection methods, and datasets.
*   **Data Leakage Mitigation:** The creation and use of the MCMD-New dataset demonstrates a strong awareness of potential data leakage issues and provides a more reliable evaluation.
*   **In-depth Analysis:** The root cause analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations of LLMs for commit message generation and suggests potential avenues for improvement.
*   **Practical Guidance:** The findings on prompt engineering and demonstration selection offer practical guidance for practitioners seeking to apply LLMs to this task.
*   **LLM as Evaluator:** The exploration and validation of LLM-based evaluation provide a cost-effective alternative to human evaluation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Root Cause Analysis:**  While the root cause analysis is valuable, it is based on a relatively small sample of 200 underperforming cases. A larger sample size might reveal additional insights.
*   **API Dependency:** The study relies on closed-source LLMs accessible through APIs. This reliance limits reproducibility and raises concerns about long-term availability and cost.  The authors mitigate this somewhat by including open-source models.
*   **Simplified Setting:**  The paper focuses primarily on generating commit messages based solely on code diffs. In real-world scenarios, developers often have access to additional information, such as issue trackers and pull requests, which could be used to generate more informative commit messages. While limiting the input this way provides a baseline, and allows easier comparisons between models, it does not reflect real-world use cases.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above strengths and weaknesses, the paper demonstrates moderate novelty and significance. The comprehensive empirical evaluation, mitigation of data leakage concerns, in-depth analysis, and practical guidance contribute to the field. However, the reliance on APIs and limited scope of root cause analysis limit the study's impact.  The authors attempt to mitigate the reliance on APIs by testing the new dataset on open-source models. The novelty is also limited as the general use of LLMs in software engineering is growing rapidly. While the application to commit messages and the study of different ICL configurations have novelty, it could have also provided more insight on using supplementary tools.

### A Pipeline of Augmentation and Sequence Embedding for Classification of Imbalanced Network Traffic
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18909v1)
- **Authors**: Matin Shokri, Ramin Hasibi
- **Abstract**: Network Traffic Classification (NTC) is one of the most important tasks in network management. The imbalanced nature of classes on the internet presents a critical challenge in classification tasks. For example, some classes of applications are much more prevalent than others, such as HTTP. As a result, machine learning classification models do not perform well on those classes with fewer data. To address this problem, we propose a pipeline to balance the dataset and classify it using a robust and accurate embedding technique. First, we generate artificial data using Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks and Kernel Density Estimation (KDE). Next, we propose replacing one-hot encoding for categorical features with a novel embedding framework based on the "Flow as a Sentence" perspective, which we name FS-Embedding. This framework treats the source and destination ports, along with the packet's direction, as one word in a flow, then trains an embedding vector space based on these new features through the learning classification task. Finally, we compare our pipeline with the training of a Convolutional Recurrent Neural Network (CRNN) and Transformers, both with imbalanced and sampled datasets, as well as with the one-hot encoding approach. We demonstrate that the proposed augmentation pipeline, combined with FS-Embedding, increases convergence speed and leads to a significant reduction in the number of model parameters, all while maintaining the same performance in terms of accuracy.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the issue of imbalanced classes in network traffic classification (NTC). The authors propose a pipeline consisting of three main components: (1) data augmentation using LSTM networks and Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) to generate artificial data for under-represented classes, (2) a novel "FS-Embedding" technique that replaces one-hot encoding for categorical features (source/destination ports and packet direction) by treating network flows as "sentences" and learning embedding vectors for these "words," and (3) classification using a Transformer architecture. The paper compares the performance of this pipeline to baselines including CRNNs and Transformers with imbalanced and sampled datasets, as well as the one-hot encoding approach, demonstrating improved convergence speed, reduced model parameters, and maintained accuracy with the proposed pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

Here's a rigorous evaluation of the paper's contribution:

*Strengths:*

*   **Addressing a Real Problem:** Imbalanced datasets are a persistent challenge in NTC and many other machine learning applications. The paper directly tackles this issue.
*   **Combined Approach:**  The pipeline combining augmentation with a novel embedding technique is a reasonable strategy. Augmentation aims to balance the data, while the FS-Embedding is designed to better capture the semantic information inherent in network flows.
*   **FS-Embedding Concept:** The "Flow as a Sentence" metaphor is potentially useful for capturing the sequential characteristics of network traffic. It's an interesting idea to apply word embedding techniques from NLP to NTC.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The paper does a good job of comparing different methods. The experimental setup includes comparisons with baselines such as CRNNs and Transformers with different configurations (imbalanced, sampled, augmented data) and comparison of FS Embedding with One Hot encoding. This provides a reasonable ground for assessing their method.

*Weaknesses and Areas for Improvement:*

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the individual components (LSTM augmentation, KDE, Transformers) are well-established, the *combination* is where the novelty lies. The individual techniques are incremental, and it remains to be seen if the combination adds significantly more value than simply using each component.
*   **FS-Embedding Deep Dive:**  The core novelty claim, FS-Embedding, lacks a deep, fundamental explanation for how and why it performs better than one-hot encoding. The justification hinges on the "Flow as a Sentence" metaphor, but the paper needs to provide more evidence that the learned embeddings actually capture meaningful semantic relationships. Visualizations or analyses of the embedding space could strengthen this. The paper needs to clarify how the frequency-based port character mapping avoids bias or information loss. Some ports might be heavily used across different applications, causing collisions. The paper did not provide details on how to address this.
*   **Limited Generality:** While the authors use a reasonable dataset, the performance gains might be specific to this dataset. The effectiveness of FS-Embedding will likely depend on the characteristics of the network traffic and the types of applications being classified. More extensive evaluation on diverse datasets would improve confidence in the approach.
*   **Overclaimed Impact:** The paper claims a "significant reduction" in model parameters. While parameter reduction is good, the actual impact and consequences of this reduction need to be carefully considered. If parameter reduction comes at the expense of performance or generalizability, then the advantage is less meaningful.
*   **Baseline Comparison:** The baseline models are not completely optimized. The CRNN and transformer baselines are not very strong for real-world traffic traces. While comparisons are made to CRNN and transformers with and without sampling, further consideration must be given to the selection and tuning of the baseline methods.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The paper lacks ablation studies. It needs to evaluate the contribution of each component of the proposed method to the final performance. E.g., How much does the augmentation contribute? How much does the FS-Embedding contribute?
*   **Interpretability:** The paper doesn't address the interpretability aspect. Can the learned FS-Embeddings provide any insights into network traffic behavior or application characteristics? Enhancing interpretability can increase the practical value of the method.

*Significance:*

The paper contributes to the ongoing research on NTC, particularly in addressing the challenges of imbalanced data and feature representation. The "Flow as a Sentence" metaphor and FS-Embedding have the potential to inspire further work in applying NLP techniques to network traffic analysis.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valid approach to addressing imbalanced data and feature representation in network traffic classification. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, and certain claims need further validation. FS-Embedding is interesting but needs a deeper analysis to convincingly demonstrate its benefits. The experimental results are promising, but the conclusions are limited by the scope of the dataset and the baselines used. The lack of ablation studies makes it difficult to determine the contribution of each component.

Therefore, taking all the strengths and weaknesses into consideration, this paper warrants a score of:

**Score: 6**

The paper represents a solid contribution, but its limited novelty, the need for more in-depth analysis of FS-Embedding, and the lack of robust justification for the performance advantages prevent it from scoring higher. The ideas are interesting and warrant further exploration and improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### CLLoRA: An Approach to Measure the Effects of the Context Length for LLM Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18910v1)
- **Authors**: Ping Zhang, Zhaorui Zhang, Sheng Di, Yao Xin, Benben Liu
- **Abstract**: Large language model fine-tuning has been identified as an efficient approach to applying the pre-trained Large language models to other domains. To guarantee data privacy for different data owners, models are often fine-tuned in federated learning environments across different data owners, which often involve data heterogeneity issues and affect the fine-tuning performance. In addition, the length of the context for the training data has been identified as a major factor that affects the LLM's model performance. To efficiently measure how the context length affects the LLM's model performance in heterogeneous federated learning environments, we propose CLLoRA. CLLoRA utilizes the parameter-efficient fine-tuning approach LoRA based on different kinds of LLMs with varying sizes as the fine-tuning approach to investigate whether the quality and length of contexts can serve as standards for measuring non-IID context. The findings indicate that an imbalance in context quality not only affects local training on clients but also impacts the global model's performance. However, context length has a minimal effect on local training but a more significant influence on the global model. These results provide insights into how context quality and length affect the model performance for LLM fine-tuning in federated learning environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Concise Summary:**

The paper "CLLORA: An Approach to Measure the Effects of the Context Length for LLM Fine-Tuning" proposes a method (CLLORA) to analyze the impact of context length on the performance of large language models (LLMs) fine-tuned in a federated learning setting.  CLLORA uses Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to fine-tune LLMs with different sizes on data partitioned according to context length using a Dirichlet distribution to simulate non-IID data. The study investigates how context length heterogeneity and quantity differences affect both local client models and the global aggregated model. The results suggest that context length imbalance impacts global model performance, while the text quantity affects client-side performances more.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** Federated learning of LLMs is a growing area, and understanding the impact of non-IID data distributions is crucial for practical deployment. The paper identifies a specific aspect of non-IID data (context length) and its influence on LLM fine-tuning performance.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The paper proposes a specific methodology (CLLORA) for measuring the impact of context length. The use of the Dirichlet distribution for synthesizing non-IID datasets with controlled context length is a reasonable approach.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper includes experiments with different LLM sizes and various levels of data imbalance, demonstrating some practical scenarios. The utilization of a federated learning framework like Plato further strengthens the practical orientation of the work.
    *   **Clear Observations:** The paper identifies key observations about the impact of context length imbalance on global model performance and the effect of data quantity variations on local client models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The core idea of using context length to define non-IID data in federated LLM fine-tuning is incremental. Existing works have explored other forms of non-IID data in federated learning and LoRA itself is a well-established PEFT technique. Utilizing context length as a feature to split data is a fairly straightforward application of existing non-IID data generation techniques.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The paper uses "textgenerator-ds-mini" as the dataset which is insufficient for drawing strong conclusions about real-world scenarios. Experiments on more diverse and larger datasets are needed to validate the findings. The nature of the text data within the dataset and its relationship with the LLM architecture could also be explored.
    *   **Oversimplification of Context:** The paper simplifies the notion of "context" to just its length. Context quality, topic, and complexity are important aspects of the text that need to be included in the investigation.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Insights:** The paper identifies the impact of context length, but it lacks deeper explanations about *why* these effects occur. A more detailed analysis of the inner workings of the LLM during fine-tuning could enhance the value.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper identifies performance differences, it does not suggest any concrete strategies for mitigating the negative impacts of context length imbalance or data quantity variations.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides empirical evidence on how context length affects LLM fine-tuning in federated learning. This could inform future research and development efforts in this area, particularly in designing more robust federated learning algorithms.
    *   The proposed CLLORA methodology can serve as a basis for further experimentation and analysis of the impact of different data characteristics on federated LLM fine-tuning.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper addresses a relevant issue and presents a systematic approach to analyzing the impact of context length. However, the novelty is limited because it builds upon existing research, and the scope of the experiments is not extensive. The paper lacks more thorough analysis and mitigation strategies. While the observations made could be useful for practitioners, they don't represent a significant breakthrough in the field.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:** The paper is above average in its approach and problem definition, meriting a score above a simple pass mark. However, the limited novelty, shallow exploration of the impact, lack of deeper analysis of the outcomes, and reliance on simplistic evaluation metrics prevent it from being a high-impact paper (which would justify a higher score, e.g. 7 or more). The paper could be improved by utilizing multiple datasets, exploring the effect of context in more detail, exploring the reasons for performance degradation or enhancement, and proposing methods to handle the identified problems.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 5/10

### END: Early Noise Dropping for Efficient and Effective Context Denoising
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18915v1)
- **Authors**: Hongye Jin, Pei Chen, Jingfeng Yang, Zhengyang Wang, Meng Jiang, Yifan Gao, Binxuan Huang, Xinyang Zhang, Zheng Li, Tianyi Liu, Huasheng Li, Bing Yin
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance across a wide range of natural language processing tasks. However, they are often distracted by irrelevant or noisy context in input sequences that degrades output quality. This problem affects both long- and short-context scenarios, such as retrieval-augmented generation, table question-answering, and in-context learning. We reveal that LLMs can implicitly identify whether input sequences contain useful information at early layers, prior to token generation. Leveraging this insight, we introduce Early Noise Dropping (\textsc{END}), a novel approach to mitigate this issue without requiring fine-tuning the LLMs. \textsc{END} segments input sequences into chunks and employs a linear prober on the early layers of LLMs to differentiate between informative and noisy chunks. By discarding noisy chunks early in the process, \textsc{END} preserves critical information, reduces distraction, and lowers computational overhead. Extensive experiments demonstrate that \textsc{END} significantly improves both performance and efficiency across different LLMs on multiple evaluation datasets. Furthermore, by investigating LLMs' implicit understanding to the input with the prober, this work also deepens understanding of how LLMs do reasoning with contexts internally.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "END: Early Noise Dropping for Efficient and Effective Context Denoising":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Early Noise Dropping" (END), a novel method to improve the performance and efficiency of Large Language Models (LLMs) when processing noisy or irrelevant context.  END segments input sequences into chunks and uses a linear prober, trained on early layers of the LLM, to classify chunks as informative or noisy.  Noisy chunks are discarded, and only informative chunks are processed further, reducing computational overhead and improving performance. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of END across various LLMs and datasets, showing improvements in both accuracy and efficiency. Crucially, END doesn't require fine-tuning, leveraging existing LLM capabilities. The authors also investigate and find that LLMs have internal mechanisms for noise discrimination at early layers that can be tapped into via a simple linear probe.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novelty:** The idea of proactively identifying and dropping noisy context chunks *before* full processing is a relatively novel approach. While context selection methods exist, END proposes leveraging the *internal representations* of the LLM *early on* to make this decision.  It avoids the more common approaches of training new components or extensive fine-tuning.
    * **Practicality:** The method is computationally efficient and can be implemented without requiring LLM fine-tuning, which makes it readily applicable to existing models.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides a robust set of experiments across different LLMs and datasets, including synthetic (NoisyRetrieval) and real-world QA datasets (NaturalQA, TriviaQA).  The performance gains and efficiency improvements are clearly demonstrated compared to strong baselines like standard RAG and an "LLM-Discrim" approach.
    * **Insightful Analysis:**  The layer-wise analysis of the prober performance is insightful, revealing that the noise discrimination capabilities emerge early in the LLM's processing.  The analysis of linear prober using randomized LLMs reveals that the prober is not just learning superficial features, but rather tapping into pre-trained LLM's innate capabilities. The probing experiments with different prompt formats and with the distinguishing query provide extra insights into LLMs' capacity.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Linear Prober Limitation:** The choice of a linear prober is simple and efficient, but it might limit the ability to capture more complex relationships between context and the query. More sophisticated probing techniques could potentially yield better results. The discussion on the limited generalization of the linear prober is also a weakness.
    * **Chunk Size Sensitivity:**  The optimal chunk size is likely to be task and model dependent, and the paper does not deeply explore the impact of different chunk sizes.  This could be an important hyperparameter to tune.
    * **Synthetic Dataset Reliance:** While real-world QA datasets are used, the NoisyRetrieval dataset is synthetic.  It's important to be cautious when extrapolating results from synthetic datasets to real-world scenarios, where noise patterns could be much more complex.
    * **Limited Large-Scale Evaluation:** While Llama3-70B is used, more extensive evaluations of very large LLMs would strengthen the paper. The lack of extensive experiments on the largest LLMs is mainly due to limited computational resources, according to the paper.

* **Significance:**

   * **Enhanced LLM Efficiency:** The ability to reduce computational overhead by dropping noisy context chunks has significant implications for deploying LLMs, especially for long-context applications.
   * **Improved Accuracy:** The improvement in accuracy by filtering out noise is valuable, especially in applications where reliability is critical.
   * **Understanding LLM Internals:**  The work contributes to a better understanding of how LLMs process context and identify relevant information.  This knowledge can inform the design of future LLM architectures and training methods.
   * **Potential Impact:** This work opens up a new avenue for exploring how we can tap into LLMs' existing capabilities and make them more robust to noise without relying on resource-intensive training methods. The work is quite general in that is easily applicable to any retrieval-augmented setting, without being tied to a specific architecture or task.

* **Justification for Score:**

   I'm assigning a score of **7**.  The paper presents a novel and practical approach to addressing a key problem in LLM applications: noise in the context. The empirical results are compelling, and the analysis is insightful.  The limitations related to the prober and chunk size are valid, but they don't significantly detract from the overall contribution. While not a groundbreaking paradigm shift, this work represents a significant and valuable step towards more efficient and reliable LLMs. It provides a practical solution with clear benefits and contributes to a better understanding of how LLMs internally process information. The biggest downside is limited exploration of very large models and somewhat simple probing technique.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ClassInvGen: Class Invariant Synthesis using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18917v1)
- **Authors**: Chuyue Sun, Viraj Agashe, Saikat Chakraborty, Jubi Taneja, Clark Barrett, David Dill, Xiaokang Qiu, Shuvendu K. Lahiri
- **Abstract**: Formal program specifications in the form of preconditions, postconditions, and class invariants have several benefits for the construction and maintenance of programs. They not only aid in program understanding due to their unambiguous semantics but can also be enforced dynamically (or even statically when the language supports a formal verifier). However, synthesizing high-quality specifications in an underlying programming language is limited by the expressivity of the specifications or the need to express them in a declarative manner. Prior work has demonstrated the potential of large language models (LLMs) for synthesizing high-quality method pre/postconditions for Python and Java, but does not consider class invariants. In this work, we describe ClassInvGen, a method for co-generating executable class invariants and test inputs to produce high-quality class invariants for a mainstream language such as C++, leveraging LLMs' ability to synthesize pure functions. We show that ClassInvGen outperforms a pure LLM-based technique to generate specifications (from code) as well as prior data-driven invariant inference techniques such as Daikon. We contribute a benchmark of standard C++ data structures along with a harness that can help measure both the correctness and completeness of generated specifications using tests and mutants. We also demonstrate its applicability to real-world code by performing a case study on several classes within a widely used and high-integrity C++ codebase.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "ClassInvGen: Class Invariant Synthesis using Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ClassInvGen, a novel method for automatically synthesizing class invariants in C++ using Large Language Models (LLMs). ClassInvGen co-generates executable class invariants and test inputs, leveraging LLMs' ability to synthesize pure functions. The approach combines static analysis with LLM-based code generation and test case generation to heuristically prune incorrect invariant candidates. The authors evaluate ClassInvGen on a benchmark of standard C++ data structures and real-world code from the Z3 SMT solver codebase, demonstrating its effectiveness compared to pure LLM-based techniques and traditional dynamic invariant inference (Daikon). The paper contributes a new benchmark, techniques for invariant-test co-generation, and a case study showcasing the applicability to real-world, high-integrity code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a solid engineering contribution to the field of automated program specification. The idea of using LLMs to generate class invariants is interesting, given their ability to reason about code structure and semantics. The key strengths of the work lie in:

*   **Practical Application:** The focus on C++ and real-world examples (Z3) increases the relevance of the work. It addresses a genuine need for automating the generation of specifications in a widely used language.
*   **Invariant-Test Co-generation:** The approach of co-generating test inputs with invariants is a good strategy to address the potential for LLMs to generate incorrect specifications. The use of line coverage to select "filtering tests" is a reasonable heuristic for guiding this process.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The authors provide a thorough empirical evaluation, comparing against LLM-only approaches and Daikon. The benchmark construction is a valuable contribution in itself. The mutation testing framework provides a quantitative measure of invariant completeness.
*   **Real-World Case Study:** The Z3 case study is convincing, showing that ClassInvGen can generate useful invariants for a complex, high-integrity codebase. The validation by a Z3 author adds weight to the results.

However, the paper also has weaknesses:

*   **Limited Novelty:** While the *combination* of techniques is novel, the individual components are not groundbreaking. LLM-based code generation and dynamic invariant inference are established areas. The core innovation is in *how* these are combined, rather than inventing new fundamental techniques. It builds upon existing work like nl2postcond.
*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The performance of ClassInvGen is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLM (GPT-4o in this case). While this is understandable, it also makes the results somewhat fragile and subject to change as LLMs evolve. Future LLMs may or may not yield similar benefits.
*   **Heuristics and Limitations:** The pruning strategy based on line coverage is a heuristic and may not always be optimal. The reliance on automatically generated tests also introduces the possibility of spurious tests that may prune valuable invariants. The context window limitation of LLMs is acknowledged, but still remains a practical concern when dealing with very large codebases.
*   **Completeness Metric:** While mutation testing provides a quantitative measure of completeness, it's an imperfect proxy. Some mutants may be equivalent, or only affect internal state not observable through public methods.
*   **Validation Challenge:** The need for manual review and validation of generated invariants by developers is a significant limitation for widespread adoption, especially in high-assurance settings, since the generation is not formally guaranteed to be safe and correct.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of automated program specification, offering a practical and effective approach for synthesizing class invariants in C++ using LLMs. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the way they are combined and applied, along with the thorough empirical evaluation and real-world case study, makes this a worthwhile contribution. The work pushes forward the boundaries of automating specification generation in an important language and codebase. However, it's crucial to acknowledge the dependence on LLM capabilities, heuristic nature of the test suite, and reliance of developers to validate correctness.

**Score: 7**

The paper's overall value stems from its demonstration that LLMs can be effectively combined with existing techniques to produce useful program specifications. While the novelty of the individual techniques is low, the contribution lies in the engineering of this new system, ClassInvGen. With developers manually validating and testing generated invariants, this is potentially a step towards higher levels of code correctness, however, future work should involve more effort toward formally verifiable approaches and more concrete guarantees of safety.

- **Classification**: cs.PL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Talking like Piping and Instrumentation Diagrams (P&IDs)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18928v1)
- **Authors**: Achmad Anggawirya Alimin, Dominik P. Goldstein, Lukas Schulze Balhorn, Artur M. Schweidtmann
- **Abstract**: We propose a methodology that allows communication with Piping and Instrumentation Diagrams (P&IDs) using natural language. In particular, we represent P&IDs through the DEXPI data model as labeled property graphs and integrate them with Large Language Models (LLMs). The approach consists of three main parts: 1) P&IDs are cast into a graph representation from the DEXPI format using our pyDEXPI Python package. 2) A tool for generating P&ID knowledge graphs from pyDEXPI. 3) Integration of the P&ID knowledge graph to LLMs using graph-based retrieval augmented generation (graph-RAG). This approach allows users to communicate with P&IDs using natural language. It extends LLM's ability to retrieve contextual data from P&IDs and mitigate hallucinations. Leveraging the LLM's large corpus, the model is also able to interpret process information in PIDs, which could help engineers in their daily tasks. In the future, this work will also open up opportunities in the context of other generative Artificial Intelligence (genAI) solutions on P&IDs, and AI-assisted HAZOP studies.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a methodology for enabling natural language interaction with Piping and Instrumentation Diagrams (P&IDs) using Large Language Models (LLMs). The approach involves converting P&IDs from the DEXPI data model into knowledge graphs, condensing these graphs into high-level representations, and then using graph-based retrieval augmented generation (RAG) to provide contextual input to LLMs. This allows users to query P&IDs using natural language, extending the LLM's ability to retrieve relevant data and mitigating hallucinations. The authors demonstrate the approach on a DEXPI P&ID sample and evaluate the performance using pattern recognition, search completeness, and knowledge inference metrics. They compare the performance of different LLMs and different knowledge graph representations (complete vs. high-level).

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a practical and potentially impactful problem: improving access to information contained within P&IDs. The use of LLMs to enable natural language interaction represents a compelling approach to a complex, manually intensive engineering task. The paper is well-structured and the methodology is clearly explained. Specifically, converting P&IDs into knowledge graphs is a logical approach, and the authors make a well-reasoned choice to use a label property graph implemented using the Neo4j framework. The steps involved in creating the high-level knowledge graph are well-defined and justified by the need to reduce the context length for the LLMs. Moreover, the evaluation process is clearly defined, using a range of questions covering different aspects of LLM performance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The proposed approach addresses a genuine pain point in process engineering.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The steps involved in the methodology are described in sufficient detail.
*   **Use of DEXPI:** Leveraging the DEXPI standard enhances the potential for interoperability and wider adoption. The use of the authors' own `pyDEXPI` package to make the data processing pipeline is appreciated.
*   **RAG Implementation:** The choice of a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) architecture is a good architectural decision as it helps provide context to the LLM which, in turn, avoids hallucination.
*   **Evaluation:** The paper presents a structured evaluation, comparing several LLMs and contrasting complete with high-level knowledge graphs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is based on a single, albeit representative, DEXPI P&ID. While the demonstration with the DEXPI P&ID is impressive, a more robust evaluation involving more real-world examples would significantly strengthen the paper.
*   **Hallucinations and Robustness:** Although the authors acknowledged the issue, the paper doesn't provide a solution for avoiding hallucinations, even with high-level graphs and RAG architecture. Further experiments should focus on the reliability aspect (e.g. generating correct results 99% of the time) of the proposed solution. The evaluation does not quantify the improvement in hallucination reduction when using RAG.
*   **Lack of Fine-Tuning:** The LLMs used were not fine-tuned specifically for P&ID interpretation. Fine-tuning might lead to substantially better performance, especially for smaller LLMs. Although this introduces extra cost/complexity, this should be mentioned as a possible next step. The current results are largely indicative of the LLM's size and pre-training, not necessarily of the effectiveness of the knowledge graph representation.
*   **Missing Implementation Details:** The exact prompts engineered and utilized for LLMs are not provided, and how they interact with the RAG architecture is not entirely clear. Similarly, it is unclear how the embedding model is selected and implemented.
*   **Comparability to Other Tools:** The paper lacks comparison to other tools for P&ID information extraction (if any exist). This makes it difficult to assess the relative advantages of the proposed approach.
*   **Limited Novelty in AI:** The AI aspect of this paper is standard RAG pipeline using LLM. The only novel aspect is the usage of RAG on P&ID.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The primary novelty lies in the application of LLMs and knowledge graphs to the specific domain of P&IDs. While knowledge graphs and LLMs are not novel technologies in themselves, their application to this particular problem is valuable. The paper demonstrates a practical and well-defined workflow for accessing information that would otherwise require significant manual effort. However, the limited scope and the lack of a comparison to alternative methods limit the overall significance.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel approach to a practical problem and demonstrates a working methodology. The strengths lie in the clear presentation and the use of relevant technologies. However, the limited scope of the evaluation, lack of fine-tuning, and lack of comparative analysis to other methodologies weaken the paper's impact. The paper serves as a good proof-of-concept, but requires more extensive validation and refinement before it can be considered a significant contribution to the field. A score of 6 reflects the potential impact but also acknowledges the limitations in the current implementation and evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### JailBench: A Comprehensive Chinese Security Assessment Benchmark for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18935v1)
- **Authors**: Shuyi Liu, Simiao Cui, Haoran Bu, Yuming Shang, Xi Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across various applications, highlighting the urgent need for comprehensive safety evaluations. In particular, the enhanced Chinese language proficiency of LLMs, combined with the unique characteristics and complexity of Chinese expressions, has driven the emergence of Chinese-specific benchmarks for safety assessment. However, these benchmarks generally fall short in effectively exposing LLM safety vulnerabilities. To address the gap, we introduce JailBench, the first comprehensive Chinese benchmark for evaluating deep-seated vulnerabilities in LLMs, featuring a refined hierarchical safety taxonomy tailored to the Chinese context. To improve generation efficiency, we employ a novel Automatic Jailbreak Prompt Engineer (AJPE) framework for JailBench construction, which incorporates jailbreak techniques to enhance assessing effectiveness and leverages LLMs to automatically scale up the dataset through context-learning. The proposed JailBench is extensively evaluated over 13 mainstream LLMs and achieves the highest attack success rate against ChatGPT compared to existing Chinese benchmarks, underscoring its efficacy in identifying latent vulnerabilities in LLMs, as well as illustrating the substantial room for improvement in the security and trustworthiness of LLMs within the Chinese context. Our benchmark is publicly available at https://github.com/STAIR-BUPT/JailBench.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "JailBench: A Comprehensive Chinese Security Assessment Benchmark for Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JailBench, a new Chinese-specific benchmark designed to evaluate the security and safety vulnerabilities of large language models (LLMs). JailBench features a refined hierarchical safety taxonomy tailored to the Chinese context, an automated prompt generation framework called Automatic Jailbreak Prompt Engineer (AJPE) to enhance assessing effectiveness and improve generation efficiency, and a dataset of 10,800 jailbreak-enhanced questions. The benchmark is evaluated on 13 mainstream LLMs, demonstrating a higher attack success rate against ChatGPT compared to existing Chinese benchmarks. The authors argue that JailBench effectively identifies latent vulnerabilities in LLMs and highlights the need for improved security and trustworthiness within the Chinese context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Chinese-Specific Focus:** Addressing the gap in safety evaluations that specifically target the Chinese language and cultural context is a significant strength. Existing benchmarks often fall short in this area.
    *   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The refined hierarchical safety taxonomy with 5 domains and 40 risk types is a valuable contribution, offering a more structured and detailed approach to assessing LLM vulnerabilities compared to some existing benchmarks.
    *   **Automated Prompt Generation (AJPE):** The AJPE framework is a novel method for automatically generating jailbreak prompts, which can potentially accelerate the development and evaluation of LLMs. It incorporates jailbreak techniques and leverages LLMs for context-aware prompt generation, making it potentially more effective and efficient than manual approaches.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on 13 LLMs, demonstrating the effectiveness of JailBench in identifying vulnerabilities. The high attack success rate against ChatGPT compared to existing Chinese benchmarks is compelling evidence.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured Paper:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly structured, making it easy to understand the methodology and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on LLMs for Prompt Generation:** The AJPE framework relies on LLMs for generating prompts, which may introduce biases or limitations depending on the underlying LLMs used. The quality and diversity of the generated prompts are directly tied to the capabilities of the LLMs used to create them. Further discussion about the potential biases and limitations introduced by this methodology is required.
    *   **Generalizability of AJPE:** While AJPE demonstrates high ASR, it is unclear how well it can generalize beyond Chinese LLMs. The Chinese-specific content may limit its usefulness in other languages.
    *   **Limited Details on Qualitative Analysis:** While quantitative results are presented, a deeper qualitative analysis of the types of vulnerabilities uncovered by JailBench would strengthen the paper. For example, specific examples of successful jailbreak prompts and the corresponding vulnerabilities could provide valuable insights.
    *   **Novelty of Individual Components:** While the combination of the Chinese-specific context, comprehensive taxonomy, and automated prompt generation is novel, the individual components (e.g., jailbreak techniques, prompt engineering) are not entirely new. The primary contribution is in the integrated framework.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of safer and more reliable LLMs within the Chinese context. By providing a comprehensive benchmark and an automated prompt generation framework, it can facilitate more rigorous testing and identification of vulnerabilities. The Chinese-specific focus is particularly important given the unique linguistic and cultural aspects. However, its impact is constrained by its focus on Chinese context, limiting its broader applications.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, I assign a score of **7**. Here's why:

*   The paper makes a tangible contribution by addressing a critical gap in LLM safety evaluations within the Chinese context.
*   The combined approach of a detailed taxonomy and an automated prompt generation technique is a worthwhile innovation.
*   The empirical results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of JailBench compared to existing benchmarks.
*   The weaknesses, such as the dependence on LLMs for prompt generation and the limited generalizability of AJPE, temper the overall score. While the paper provides a comprehensive benchmark, there are also other benchmarks (e.g. comprehensive benchmarks for code) that have made greater contribution, indicating that this paper's novelty and significance is not as high as those benchmarks.
*   The significance is moderately high, but its impact on the overall LLM safety field (outside the Chinese context) is more limited.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Label-Only Membership Inference Attack against Pre-trained Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18943v1)
- **Authors**: Yu He, Boheng Li, Liu Liu, Zhongjie Ba, Wei Dong, Yiming Li, Zhan Qin, Kui Ren, Chun Chen
- **Abstract**: Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs) aim to predict whether a data sample belongs to the model's training set or not. Although prior research has extensively explored MIAs in Large Language Models (LLMs), they typically require accessing to complete output logits (\ie, \textit{logits-based attacks}), which are usually not available in practice. In this paper, we study the vulnerability of pre-trained LLMs to MIAs in the \textit{label-only setting}, where the adversary can only access generated tokens (text). We first reveal that existing label-only MIAs have minor effects in attacking pre-trained LLMs, although they are highly effective in inferring fine-tuning datasets used for personalized LLMs. We find that their failure stems from two main reasons, including better generalization and overly coarse perturbation. Specifically, due to the extensive pre-training corpora and exposing each sample only a few times, LLMs exhibit minimal robustness differences between members and non-members. This makes token-level perturbations too coarse to capture such differences. To alleviate these problems, we propose \textbf{PETAL}: a label-only membership inference attack based on \textbf{PE}r-\textbf{T}oken sem\textbf{A}ntic simi\textbf{L}arity. Specifically, PETAL leverages token-level semantic similarity to approximate output probabilities and subsequently calculate the perplexity. It finally exposes membership based on the common assumption that members are `better' memorized and have smaller perplexity. We conduct extensive experiments on the WikiMIA benchmark and the more challenging MIMIR benchmark. Empirically, our PETAL performs better than the extensions of existing label-only attacks against personalized LLMs and even on par with other advanced logit-based attacks across all metrics on five prevalent open-source LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses membership inference attacks (MIAs) against pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) in a label-only setting, where the adversary only has access to generated text, not output logits. The authors reveal that existing label-only MIAs are largely ineffective due to better generalization and overly coarse perturbation at the token level in pre-trained LLMs.  To overcome this, they propose PETAL, a novel label-only MIA exploiting per-token semantic similarity to approximate output probabilities and determine membership based on perplexity. Through extensive experiments, the authors demonstrate that PETAL significantly outperforms existing label-only attacks and achieves comparable performance to logits-based attacks. They also analyze the impact of various parameters on PETAL's performance and evaluate the effectiveness of common defenses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in effectively executing MIAs against *pre-trained* LLMs in a *label-only* setting. Prior works primarily focused on logits-based attacks against LLMs, or label-only attacks against fine-tuned models. The paper identifies and addresses the specific challenges presented by pre-trained LLMs regarding generalization and perturbation granularity. The proposed PETAL attack, leveraging token-level semantic similarity for membership inference, is a fresh approach. The idea of focusing on the first generated token is also a meaningful contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a practical vulnerability of LLMs even in realistic, restrictive settings. This is significant because many commercial LLM APIs only expose generated text. Demonstrating that MIAs are still possible without access to logits raises serious privacy concerns. The paper calls for increased attention to developing more robust defenses and demonstrates the inadequacy of some existing defense strategies. The case studies on closed-source models are a valuable contribution, highlighting the issue in real-world commercial deployments.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, proposed solution, and experimental setup.
    *   The experimental evaluation is extensive, covering multiple datasets, models, and attack configurations. The ablation studies and parameter analyses provide valuable insights.
    *   The analysis of why existing label-only attacks fail against pre-trained LLMs is insightful.
    *   The exploration of defenses is a valuable addition, showing the limitations of some commonly used techniques.
    *   The paper explores and exposes vulnerabilities within closed source models, which adds to the real-world applicability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a surrogate model is a potential limitation. While the paper argues that the mapping between semantic similarity and probabilities is relatively consistent across models, the choice of the surrogate can still influence results. The paper addresses this by testing different surrogate models.
    *   While the paper touches upon defenses, this section could be expanded. A more thorough evaluation of advanced defenses, specifically designed for LLMs, would further strengthen the contribution.
    *   The computational cost of PETAL, although addressed, could be a barrier for some practical applications, especially on extremely long sequences. The cost-benefit analysis might not always be advantageous compared to the effectiveness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the research community to focus on more practical MIA scenarios and develop more effective defense strategies specifically tailored to LLMs. The findings will likely motivate model developers to consider label-only attacks when evaluating the privacy risks of their systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** While the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of MIA research against LLMs, it is not without limitations. The proposed PETAL attack is effective and addresses a practical gap, but reliance on surrogate models and potential computational overhead limit its widespread adoption. The defense analysis could be more in-depth. However, its insightful analysis of the problem, successful development of PETAL and thorough experimental work, including against closed source models, make it an impactful work and will likely lead to future research and potentially impact the deployment strategies of LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Switching multiplicative watermark design against covert attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18948v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander J. Gallo, Sribalaji C. Anand, André M. H. Teixeira, Riccardo M. G. Ferrari
- **Abstract**: Active techniques have been introduced to give better detectability performance for cyber-attack diagnosis in cyber-physical systems (CPS). In this paper, switching multiplicative watermarking is considered, whereby we propose an optimal design strategy to define switching filter parameters. Optimality is evaluated exploiting the so-called output-to-output gain of the closed loop system, including some supposed attack dynamics. A worst-case scenario of a matched covert attack is assumed, presuming that an attacker with full knowledge of the closed-loop system injects a stealthy attack of bounded energy. Our algorithm, given watermark filter parameters at some time instant, provides optimal next-step parameters. Analysis of the algorithm is given, demonstrating its features, and demonstrating that through initialization of certain parameters outside of the algorithm, the parameters of the multiplicative watermarking can be randomized. Simulation shows how, by adopting our method for parameter design, the attacker's impact on performance diminishes.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a method for designing switching multiplicative watermarking (mWM) filters in cyber-physical systems (CPS) to enhance security against covert attacks. It focuses on optimally designing the mWM filter parameters after a switching event, considering a worst-case scenario where an attacker has full knowledge of the system. The paper formulates the problem as an optimization problem minimizing the attack-energy-constrained output-to-output gain (AEC-OOG). It demonstrates the feasibility of the optimization problem based on stability conditions and suggests a solution to randomize the mWM filter parameters to hinder eavesdropping attackers. The method’s effectiveness is illustrated with a numerical example.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The idea of using switching multiplicative watermarking isn't entirely new, as previous work by Ferrari and Teixeira (2021) has already introduced this concept.
    *   The use of AEC-OOG as an optimization objective is also built on previous work by Anand and Teixeira (2023)
    *   The main novelty lies in *optimally designing the mWM filter parameters specifically after a switching event, considering a covert attacker with full knowledge of the system parameters and using AEC-OOG as an optimality metric.* This specific problem formulation and the proposed optimization algorithm build on previous works but provide further enhancement.
    *   The randomization strategy, while not groundbreaking, contributes to addressing the eavesdropping attack.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses an important problem: securing CPS against increasingly sophisticated cyberattacks. Watermarking techniques offer a potentially valuable defense mechanism.
    *   The consideration of a covert attacker with full system knowledge is a strong point, making the analysis more realistic and relevant. This "worst-case" assumption strengthens the robustness of the proposed design.
    *   The use of AEC-OOG as an objective function provides a quantitative measure of the performance degradation due to attacks, allowing for a more principled design of the watermarking filters.
    *   However, the paper focuses specifically on additive attacks injected between the plant and the controller and may not be directly applicable to other types of attacks.
    *   Moreover, the exhaustive search algorithm proposed to solve the non-convex optimization problem is computationally expensive and may not scale well to large-scale systems or systems with higher dimensional filter parameter spaces. Its suitability for online, real-time adaptation in realistic CPS scenarios is questionable.
    *   It can be questioned whether the assumption of the energy bound on the adversary’s attack is overly restrictive; in many scenarios, the attacker’s resources may not be so limited.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem formulation and mathematical framework.
    *   Consideration of a realistic attack scenario (covert attacker).
    *   Provides a feasibility analysis based on stability conditions.
    *   Numerical example demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   The attack-energy-constrained output-to-output gain (AEC-OOG) provides a solid metric for quantifying the performance loss due to attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The exhaustive search algorithm is computationally expensive and does not scale well.
    *   The assumption of a known energy bound for the attacker might be limiting.
    *   The analysis is limited to linear time-invariant (LTI) systems and additive attacks.
    *   The randomization strategy relies on synchronizing parameters across the plant and the controller, which can be challenging in practical scenarios.
    *   There is very little discussion on the practical implications of this scheme. The complexity of implementing the filters, the synchronization requirement, and the cost associated with the trade-off between performance and security are not adequately addressed.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on active attack diagnosis in CPS.
    *   The optimization framework and the AEC-OOG metric could be adapted and extended to other watermarking techniques and attack scenarios.
    *   The paper could inspire further research into more efficient algorithms for designing switching watermarking filters.
    *   The results may encourage system designers to carefully consider worst-case attacks in the design of secure control systems.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of CPS security by addressing the problem of designing switching watermarking filters. The formulation and framework build on existing works but enhances robustness against covert attackers. However, the computational complexity of the proposed solution and the restrictive assumptions limit its practical applicability and overall impact.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The score of 6 reflects the paper's moderate novelty and significance. While it builds upon previous work and introduces some specific enhancements, such as the worst-case attacker model and AEC-OOG for mWM design, the limitations of the exhaustive search algorithm and the restrictive assumptions prevent it from achieving a higher score. The theoretical framework is sound, but the practical applicability needs further investigation. The contribution is valuable, but more work is required to address the identified weaknesses and enhance the real-world deployment potential. It makes a contribution, but not a breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### DualSpec: Text-to-spatial-audio Generation via Dual-Spectrogram Guided Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18952v1)
- **Authors**: Lei Zhao, Sizhou Chen, Linfeng Feng, Xiao-Lei Zhang, Xuelong Li
- **Abstract**: Text-to-audio (TTA), which generates audio signals from textual descriptions, has received huge attention in recent years. However, recent works focused on text to monaural audio only. As we know, spatial audio provides more immersive auditory experience than monaural audio, e.g. in virtual reality. To address this issue, we propose a text-to-spatial-audio (TTSA) generation framework named DualSpec.Specifically, it first trains variational autoencoders (VAEs) for extracting the latent acoustic representations from sound event audio. Then, given text that describes sound events and event directions, the proposed method uses the encoder of a pretrained large language model to transform the text into text features. Finally, it trains a diffusion model from the latent acoustic representations and text features for the spatial audio generation. In the inference stage, only the text description is needed to generate spatial audio. Particularly, to improve the synthesis quality and azimuth accuracy of the spatial sound events simultaneously, we propose to use two kinds of acoustic features. One is the Mel spectrograms which is good for improving the synthesis quality, and the other is the short-time Fourier transform spectrograms which is good at improving the azimuth accuracy. We provide a pipeline of constructing spatial audio dataset with text prompts, for the training of the VAEs and diffusion model. We also introduce new spatial-aware evaluation metrics to quantify the azimuth errors of the generated spatial audio recordings. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method can generate spatial audio with high directional and event consistency.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DualSpec, a novel text-to-spatial-audio (TTSA) generation framework. Unlike existing text-to-audio (TTA) models that focus on monaural audio, DualSpec aims to generate immersive spatial audio directly from text descriptions. The core idea is to leverage a dual-spectrogram guided diffusion model. It utilizes two types of acoustic features: Mel spectrograms for synthesis quality and Short-Time Fourier Transform (STFT) spectrograms for azimuth accuracy. The method involves training variational autoencoders (VAEs) to extract latent representations from sound event audio, and then training a diffusion model conditioned on these latent representations and text features extracted from a pre-trained large language model (LLM). A pipeline for constructing spatial audio datasets with text prompts and new spatial-aware evaluation metrics are also introduced.  Experiments demonstrate the method's ability to generate spatial audio with directional and event consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in addressing the TTSA task directly. Existing TTA focuses on monaural audio. While AudioSpa is mentioned as a related work, it relies on monaural audio as a *reference*, not generating spatial audio solely from text. The use of *dual* spectrogram guidance (Mel and STFT) within a diffusion model is also a novel aspect for spatial audio. The combination aims to address the trade-off between synthesis quality and spatial accuracy that often plagues audio generation methods. The spatial-aware evaluation metrics also add value.

*   **Significance:** Spatial audio is crucial for applications like VR/AR, gaming, and interactive environments. A functional TTSA model would have significant implications for content creation in these domains. While the paper shows promising results, the significance is tempered by the relatively early stage of the research and the limited comparison.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:** Addresses an important and relatively unexplored area (TTSA).
    *   **Methodological Innovation:** The DualSpec architecture, with the dual-spectrogram approach and diffusion model, is well-motivated and implemented.
    *   **Data Pipeline:** The proposed pipeline for spatial audio dataset creation is valuable, as such datasets are currently scarce.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** Attempts to provide objective evaluation of spatial audio quality, which is a complex problem.
    *   **Experimental Validation:**  The experiments provide some evidence of the method's effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Baselines:** The paper primarily compares against recent *monaural* audio generation methods.
