We further construct training preference pairs using RewardAgent and train an LLM with the DPO objective, achieving superior performance on various NLP benchmarks compared to conventional reward models. Our codes are publicly released to facilitate further research (https://github.com/THU-KEG/Agentic-Reward-Modeling).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Agentic Reward Modeling," a novel reward system designed to improve the reliability of reward models (RMs) used in training and scaling large language models (LLMs).  The core idea is to augment traditional preference-based reward models with verifiable correctness signals from different aspects, like factuality and instruction following. The authors implement a "REWARDAGENT" that combines human preference rewards (using the ArmoRM model) with two verification agents: one for factuality and another for instruction following (e.g., length constraints). Experiments on existing reward model benchmarks and downstream tasks demonstrate the REWARDAGENT's superiority over vanilla reward models. Furthermore, training an LLM using preference pairs generated by REWARDAGENT yields improved performance on standard NLP benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** While reward modeling is an active area of research, integrating verifiable correctness signals as a core component alongside human preferences appears reasonably novel. Existing RMs heavily rely on human feedback, which can be subjective and biased. The proposed agentic approach is a step towards making reward functions more objective and reliable.  The design of the verification agents, particularly the pairwise factuality check, which optimizes for efficiency, demonstrates a clear attempt to address practical limitations of existing factuality evaluation methods.  However, rule-based reward signals have been explored in other contexts, so the main novelty is the *combination* of these signals within a specific architecture designed for general-purpose language modeling.

**Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a critical weakness of current LLM training paradigms: the reliance on subjective and potentially flawed human feedback. Improving the reliability of reward models directly translates to more reliable and aligned LLMs. The demonstrated improvements on existing reward benchmarks and downstream tasks support this claim. The ablation study clearly demonstrates the value that well-designed verfication agents are able to have. Furthermore, the findings suggesting that even modest, open-source models using the REWARDAGENT framework outperform larger, proprietary models are significant in terms of resource efficiency and accessibility. The availability of the code and IFBench benchmark contribute towards furthering research into the development of reward models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper effectively highlights the limitations of purely preference-based RMs and the need for verifiable correctness.
*   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The REWARDAGENT architecture, including the Router, Verification Agents, and Judger, is well-described and modular.
*   **Efficient Factuality Evaluation:** The pairwise factuality verification agent is a practical design choice for efficient reward scoring.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct thorough experiments across multiple benchmarks and downstream tasks.
*   **Demonstrated Improvements:** The empirical results consistently show REWARDAGENT's advantages.
*   **Code and Benchmark Release:** The public release of the code and the IFBench benchmark is a valuable contribution to the research community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs for Verification:**  While the pairwise factuality check is more efficient, it still relies on potentially flawed LLMs for difference proposal, query generation, and verification. A more robust, knowledge-based approach to factuality could be more reliable. Note that they do attempt to deal with this via the router and only calling the specific checker when applicable. However, this is still very model dependent.
*   **Limited Scope of Verifiable Signals:** The paper primarily focuses on factuality and instruction following. Other aspects, such as safety, toxicity, or ethical considerations, could be incorporated as additional verification agents for better overall alignment. Also note that some instructions are very sensitive to the specific model and prompts that the paper doesn't evaluate against.
*   **Judger Weighting:** The Judger currently uses a simple weighted sum with equal weights. More sophisticated methods for combining correctness signals and human preferences, such as adaptive weighting or gating mechanisms, could be explored. One should explore how important those specific signals are.
*   **Limited Oracle Analysis:** The results show a performance increase in the oracle setting. Is this improvement worth the additional effort? Can you simply choose the best reward model as a baseline in some scenarios?
*   **Lack of Detail on Negative Responses:** There is a lack of detail on how specifically they determined when to label a specific sample with a hard, normal, or simple label in the rejected response.

**Score Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of reward modeling. The proposed agentic approach is a logical and well-executed step towards addressing the limitations of purely preference-based RMs. The comprehensive experiments and demonstrated improvements provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the REWARDAGENT. The public release of code and a new benchmark further enhances the paper's value.

However, the reliance on LLMs for verification and the limited scope of verifiable signals somewhat temper the overall impact. Therefore, while promising, the work is not revolutionary and has room for further refinement.

Score: 7

The paper is a valuable contribution with a clear path for future research. It demonstrates a practical and effective approach to improving the reliability of reward models, addressing a key challenge in scaling LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating LLMs and Pre-trained Models for Text Summarization Across Diverse Datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19339v1)
- **Authors**: Tohida Rehman, Soumabha Ghosh, Kuntal Das, Souvik Bhattacharjee, Debarshi Kumar Sanyal, Samiran Chattopadhyay
- **Abstract**: Text summarization plays a crucial role in natural language processing by condensing large volumes of text into concise and coherent summaries. As digital content continues to grow rapidly and the demand for effective information retrieval increases, text summarization has become a focal point of research in recent years. This study offers a thorough evaluation of four leading pre-trained and open-source large language models: BART, FLAN-T5, LLaMA-3-8B, and Gemma-7B, across five diverse datasets CNN/DM, Gigaword, News Summary, XSum, and BBC News. The evaluation employs widely recognized automatic metrics, including ROUGE-1, ROUGE-2, ROUGE-L, BERTScore, and METEOR, to assess the models' capabilities in generating coherent and informative summaries. The results reveal the comparative strengths and limitations of these models in processing various text types.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper evaluates the performance of four pre-trained language models (BART, FLAN-T5, LLaMA-3-8B, and Gemma-7B) for abstractive text summarization across five diverse datasets (CNN/DM, Gigaword, News Summary, XSum, and BBC News).  It fine-tunes the models and compares their performance using ROUGE, BERTScore, and METEOR metrics. The results highlight the comparative strengths and weaknesses of each model on different text types, also including insights on computational resources and case studies with examples. The paper further does an evaluation of the summaries using ChatGPT as a judge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. Evaluating pre-trained language models on summarization tasks is a well-trodden area. While using newer models like LLaMA-3-8B and Gemma-7B provides slightly updated results, the core methodology isn't groundbreaking. The inclusion of multiple datasets is a strength, but this has become a fairly standard practice in summarization research.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate.  The paper provides a useful comparative analysis that could guide practitioners in selecting a model for a specific dataset/application. The resource usage analysis can be helpful for those working with limited computational resources. However, the findings are not likely to drastically shift the direction of research in the field. The ChatGPT-as-a-judge evaluation, while interesting, is only conducted on a very small sample size and therefore the insight gained from it is also limited.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper uses multiple datasets and evaluation metrics, providing a relatively thorough comparison.
    *   **Resource Analysis:** The paper includes valuable information about the computational cost of fine-tuning each model.
    *   **Inclusion of Recent Models**: It evaluates more recent LLMs like LLaMA-3-8B and Gemma-7B.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Methodology:** The core approach of fine-tuning and evaluating models on standard benchmarks is not innovative.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Insights:** The paper primarily presents experimental results without delving into the underlying reasons for model performance differences. There's little theoretical analysis about why certain models excel on specific datasets.
    *   **Shallow Error Analysis:** While case studies are presented, the error analysis is surface-level. A deeper investigation into the types of errors made by each model (e.g., factual inaccuracies, coherence issues) would strengthen the analysis. The reliance on magenta, cyan and yellow color coding to indicate different errors is not helpful for print or those with visual impairments.
    *   **Limited ChatGPT Evaluation:** Using only 5 examples per dataset significantly limits the reliability of the results of evaluation via ChatGPT.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence on the field is expected to be modest. It could serve as a reference for practitioners looking to choose between the evaluated models for specific summarization tasks. However, it's unlikely to inspire new research directions or fundamental breakthroughs. The paper does not introduce any new summarization techniques or evaluation metrics.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:**

The paper is a reasonably well-executed empirical study, but it lacks significant novelty or in-depth analysis. The strengths of thorough evaluation and resource analysis are offset by the methodological limitations. While the paper provides a useful snapshot of the performance of various models on specific datasets, it doesn't offer substantial theoretical insights or innovative approaches that would significantly advance the field of text summarization. The paper does not offer a significant improvement over similar comparative studies.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Can Large Language Models Detect Errors in Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19361v1)
- **Authors**: Yancheng He, Shilong Li, Jiaheng Liu, Weixun Wang, Xingyuan Bu, Ge Zhang, Zhongyuan Peng, Zhaoxiang Zhang, Wenbo Su, Bo Zheng
- **Abstract**: Recently, o1-like models have drawn significant attention, where these models produce the long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning steps to improve the reasoning abilities of existing Large Language Models (LLMs). In this paper, to understand the qualities of these long CoTs and measure the critique abilities of existing LLMs on these long CoTs, we introduce the DeltaBench, including the generated long CoTs from different o1-like models (e.g., QwQ, DeepSeek-R1) for different reasoning tasks (e.g., Math, Code, General Reasoning), to measure the ability to detect errors in long CoT reasoning. Based on DeltaBench, we first perform fine-grained analysis of the generated long CoTs to discover the effectiveness and efficiency of different o1-like models. Then, we conduct extensive evaluations of existing process reward models (PRMs) and critic models to detect the errors of each annotated process, which aims to investigate the boundaries and limitations of existing PRMs and critic models. Finally, we hope that DeltaBench could guide developers to better understand the long CoT reasoning abilities of their models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DeltaBench, a new dataset designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to detect errors in long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. DeltaBench includes CoTs generated by various LLMs (including o1-like models) across different reasoning tasks (math, code, general reasoning, PCB). The paper provides a fine-grained analysis of the generated CoTs, examines the error detection capabilities of existing Process Reward Models (PRMs) and critic models on these CoTs, and identifies key limitations of current approaches. The authors highlight issues like fundamental errors in CoTs, low proportions of effective reflections, and redundancy in reasoning processes. They also evaluate the critique abilities of LLMs and PRMs, finding limited effectiveness in error identification, weaker self-critique abilities, and performance degradation with longer contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Dataset:** The primary strength lies in the creation of DeltaBench, a dedicated dataset specifically for evaluating error detection in long CoT reasoning. The lack of publicly available datasets for this crucial aspect makes DeltaBench a significant contribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper conducts a detailed analysis of the generated CoTs, highlighting various types of errors, redundancy, and limitations of existing models. This analysis provides valuable insights into the challenges of long CoT reasoning.
    *   **Practical Significance:** The research addresses a critical need for evaluating and improving the reliability of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks. The findings can guide developers in understanding the limitations of their models and developing more robust approaches.
    *   **Multi-faceted Evaluation:** The paper explores various evaluation metrics, beyond just overall accuracy, to paint a fuller picture of the capabilities and limitations of different models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Annotation Bias:** While the paper mentions efforts to mitigate it, the annotation process is inherently subjective and may introduce biases that influence the evaluation results. The data annotation strategy is not as detailed as one might hope, with more information relegated to an appendix which is less accessible or easily verified.
    *   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While diverse, the included reasoning tasks might not be fully representative of all complex real-world scenarios where LLMs are employed.
    *   **Potential for Benchmark Gaming:** The static nature of the benchmark poses the risk of models being specifically tuned for DeltaBench, potentially diminishing its long-term effectiveness.
    *   **Baseline Model Selection:** The choice of baseline models for evaluation could be expanded to include a broader range of architectures and training methods.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Evaluation with alternative methods for error detection:** The paper primarily focuses on the performance of LLMs and PRMs without rigorous comparison with alternative error detection methodologies.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   While individual components (long CoT generation, PRMs, LLM critique) are not entirely new, the combination of these elements into a dedicated benchmark for error detection in *long* CoT reasoning is novel.
    *   The fine-grained analysis of error types within long CoTs contributes valuable insights beyond simply assessing overall accuracy.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper tackles a growing challenge in the field: ensuring the reliability of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks. The development of DeltaBench addresses a gap in the existing evaluation landscape.
    *   The findings can inform future research directions, particularly in the development of more effective PRMs, critic models, and error-robust reasoning techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   DeltaBench has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmark for evaluating error detection in long CoT reasoning.
    *   The insights from the analysis can guide the development of more reliable and trustworthy LLMs for real-world applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the strengths and weaknesses, a score of **7** seems appropriate. DeltaBench fills an important gap in the evaluation of LLMs, providing a dedicated resource for assessing error detection capabilities in long CoT reasoning. The comprehensive analysis and practical insights are valuable contributions to the field. However, the potential for annotation bias, limited task scope, and the static nature of the benchmark slightly temper the score. Expanding on methods for bias reduction, including more detail on data annotation strategies and a more robust comparison with alternative error detection mechanisms would increase the paper's contribution significantly.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DataMan: Data Manager for Pre-training Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19363v1)
- **Authors**: Ru Peng, Kexin Yang, Yawen Zeng, Junyang Lin, Dayiheng Liu, Junbo Zhao
- **Abstract**: The performance emergence of large language models (LLMs) driven by data scaling laws makes the selection of pre-training data increasingly important. However, existing methods rely on limited heuristics and human intuition, lacking comprehensive and clear guidelines. To address this, we are inspired by ``reverse thinking'' -- prompting LLMs to self-identify which criteria benefit its performance. As its pre-training capabilities are related to perplexity (PPL), we derive 14 quality criteria from the causes of text perplexity anomalies and introduce 15 common application domains to support domain mixing. In this paper, we train a Data Manager (DataMan) to learn quality ratings and domain recognition from pointwise rating, and use it to annotate a 447B token pre-training corpus with 14 quality ratings and domain type. Our experiments validate our approach, using DataMan to select 30B tokens to train a 1.3B-parameter language model, demonstrating significant improvements in in-context learning (ICL), perplexity, and instruction-following ability over the state-of-the-art baseline. The best-performing model, based on the Overall Score l=5 surpasses a model trained with 50% more data using uniform sampling. We continue pre-training with high-rated, domain-specific data annotated by DataMan to enhance domain-specific ICL performance and thus verify DataMan's domain mixing ability. Our findings emphasize the importance of quality ranking, the complementary nature of quality criteria, and their low correlation with perplexity, analyzing misalignment between PPL and ICL performance. We also thoroughly analyzed our pre-training dataset, examining its composition, the distribution of quality ratings, and the original document sources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DataMan, a data manager for pre-training large language models (LLMs).  The core idea is to use LLMs themselves to identify and rate the quality and domain of pre-training data.  Inspired by "reverse thinking" (prompting an LLM to identify criteria that benefit its own performance, tied to perplexity), the authors derive 14 quality criteria and 15 domain categories. They train DataMan to annotate a large (447B token) pre-training corpus, use DataMan to select a smaller subset (30B tokens), and train a 1.3B parameter language model.  Experiments demonstrate improvements in in-context learning, perplexity, and instruction-following compared to baseline methods, with the best model outperforming a model trained on 50% more uniformly sampled data. The paper further explores domain-specific fine-tuning using DataMan's domain annotations and analyzes the relationship between perplexity and ICL performance.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs to evaluate the quality of their own training data is somewhat novel, but builds on prior work that uses LLMs for data selection and scoring. The specific methodology of deriving the 14 quality criteria from perplexity anomalies and then training a "Data Manager" adds to this. The comprehensive nature of the criteria, moving beyond simple heuristics, is a strength. However, using *GPT-4* (or whatever the current top LLM is) is resource-intensive, limiting reproducibility. The paper's "reverse thinking" idea is a clever framing, but in practice relies on a large language model's existing knowledge and biases, which isn't entirely a self-derived process. Using pointwise ranking and moving beyond pairwise ranking also brings a new approach.
*   **Significance:** The primary significance lies in demonstrating a practical method to improve LLM performance by intelligent data selection. The improvements demonstrated in ICL and instruction following tasks are compelling. The analysis of the dataset composition, quality rating distributions, and correlations is valuable for understanding data selection strategies. This could have a real-world impact by allowing for more efficient training of LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with comprehensive data curation and evaluation.
    *   Strong experimental results showcasing improvements over baselines.
    *   Detailed analysis of the learned criteria and the dataset, offering insights into quality assessment.
    *   Release of code, models, and the annotated DataPajama dataset promotes further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on a proprietary, closed-source LLM (GPT-4) for annotation is a major limitation. This makes the study difficult to replicate. The cost of $13,858 for just the fine-tuning dataset is exorbitant, and not every researchers can replicate this at different scales or with other models.
    *   The 1.3B parameter model used for the main experiments is relatively small by today's standards. While improvements are shown, it's unclear if these scale effectively to larger models.
    *   The process of "reverse thinking" to derive the quality criteria, while clever, is ultimately limited by the biases and knowledge of the LLM used to generate the prompt. This is still somewhat subjective.
    *  The pointwise rating, although explained adequately, still misses some important components which pairwise rating does not.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could significantly influence future research in LLM pre-training by:
    *   Encouraging the use of LLMs for automated data quality assessment and curation.
    *   Prompting the development of more comprehensive data selection criteria.
    *   Highlighting the importance of data quality ranking in addition to simply filtering or sampling.
    *   Motivating further exploration of the relationship between perplexity and downstream task performance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed methodology with promising results, adding to the growing field of LLM pre-training data selection. However, its reliance on a proprietary LLM for crucial parts of the process significantly hinders reproducibility and limits its generalizability. The relatively small model size also casts some doubt on the scalability of the benefits. Therefore, while the idea and analysis are valuable, these limitations impact its overall significance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TheoremExplainAgent: Towards Multimodal Explanations for LLM Theorem Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19400v1)
- **Authors**: Max Ku, Thomas Chong, Jonathan Leung, Krish Shah, Alvin Yu, Wenhu Chen
- **Abstract**: Understanding domain-specific theorems often requires more than just text-based reasoning; effective communication through structured visual explanations is crucial for deeper comprehension. While large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong performance in text-based theorem reasoning, their ability to generate coherent and pedagogically meaningful visual explanations remains an open challenge. In this work, we introduce TheoremExplainAgent, an agentic approach for generating long-form theorem explanation videos (over 5 minutes) using Manim animations. To systematically evaluate multimodal theorem explanations, we propose TheoremExplainBench, a benchmark covering 240 theorems across multiple STEM disciplines, along with 5 automated evaluation metrics. Our results reveal that agentic planning is essential for generating detailed long-form videos, and the o3-mini agent achieves a success rate of 93.8% and an overall score of 0.77. However, our quantitative and qualitative studies show that most of the videos produced exhibit minor issues with visual element layout. Furthermore, multimodal explanations expose deeper reasoning flaws that text-based explanations fail to reveal, highlighting the importance of multimodal explanations.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Paper Summary:**

The paper "TheoremExplainAgent: Towards Multimodal Explanations for LLM Theorem Understanding" introduces a novel framework for generating multimodal (video) explanations for theorems across various STEM disciplines. The core contribution is *TheoremExplainAgent*, an agentic AI system that plans and generates long-form explanatory videos using Manim animations. The paper also presents *TheoremExplainBench*, a new benchmark dataset with 240 theorems and proposes automated evaluation metrics to assess the quality of the generated videos across dimensions like accuracy, visual relevance, logical flow, element layout, and visual consistency.  The experimental results highlight the importance of agentic planning and demonstrate the system's ability to generate extended video explanations, while also revealing limitations in visual quality and the potential to expose reasoning flaws that text-based evaluations miss.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of the Problem Formulation:** The paper tackles a relatively unexplored problem: generating multimodal explanations of theorems. While LLMs have been applied to theorem solving, the focus on *explanation* and the use of videos is a fresh perspective. The emphasis on visual aids in understanding complex concepts is well-motivated.
    *   **Agentic Approach:** The use of an agentic framework with distinct planning and coding agents is a valuable architectural choice. It allows for more structured and coherent video generation compared to simple, single-step approaches. The use of RAG to make the agents knowledgeable on Manim's codebase is another notable design decision that can further the field of automated AI agents.
    *   **Dataset and Evaluation Metrics:** TheoremExplainBench fills a gap in the availability of datasets specifically designed for evaluating multimodal theorem explanations. The proposed evaluation metrics, while not perfect, provide a starting point for quantifying the quality of generated videos.
    *   **Insightful Error Analysis:**  The identification of error categories (Manim code hallucinations, LaTeX rendering errors, and general coding errors) offers valuable insights into the limitations of current LLMs in this domain. The paper convincingly argues that visual explanations expose deeper reasoning flaws than text-based methods. The paper clearly shows visual explanation allows users to identify not only when an output is wrong, but *why* it is wrong.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Visual Quality Limitations:** The paper acknowledges that the generated videos still exhibit visual imperfections (misaligned elements, overlapping shapes, etc.). While the agentic approach generates longer videos, the visual execution still lags behind what a skilled human animator could produce.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Shortcomings:** The automated evaluation metrics, relying on LLMs for assessment, are susceptible to biases and inaccuracies, especially in judging visual aspects. The moderate correlation with human ratings suggests room for improvement. It makes one question the objective utility of automating this process versus hiring skilled humans to do this assessment.
    *   **Limited Generality:** The framework is heavily reliant on Manim, which limits its applicability to other types of visual explanations or different domains. The evaluation only considers video output; it doesn't explore the potential of interactive or user-adaptive explanations. It may be better to focus on a general methodology of how an AI agent can use visuals to describe complex concepts instead of being too focused on how to construct Manim scripts.
    *   **Lack of comparisons:** The lack of comparison versus other methods of generating STEM education videos is a significant limitation. A comparison versus simpler approaches would better highlight the value of the approach.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Inspiration for Future Research:** The paper opens up a new avenue for research in multimodal AI and automated explanation generation. The benchmark and the identified challenges will likely stimulate further work in this area. It shows an important usage of AI agents to describe and explain difficult concepts that should not be understated.
    *   **Potential Educational Applications:** While not yet ready for direct classroom use, the work demonstrates the potential of AI to create personalized and engaging learning resources.
    *   **Advancement for AI agents:** A working prototype demonstrates the feasibility of a framework and sets a baseline for future progress.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a novel problem and introducing a comprehensive framework, it has several limitations that affect its overall significance. The visual quality issues, reliance on automated evaluation metrics, and limited generality temper the impact. The reliance on a specific library limits its generalization. However, the error analysis and the demonstration of the agentic planning approach and the newly created dataset are significant strengths. The paper successfully highlights the need for more research into generating multimodal explanations and identifies several key challenges.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### General Reasoning Requires Learning to Reason from the Get-go
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19402v1)
- **Authors**: Seungwook Han, Jyothish Pari, Samuel J. Gershman, Pulkit Agrawal
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive real-world utility, exemplifying artificial useful intelligence (AUI). However, their ability to reason adaptively and robustly -- the hallmarks of artificial general intelligence (AGI) -- remains fragile. While LLMs seemingly succeed in commonsense reasoning, programming, and mathematics, they struggle to generalize algorithmic understanding across novel contexts. Our experiments with algorithmic tasks in esoteric programming languages reveal that LLM's reasoning overfits to the training data and is limited in its transferability. We hypothesize that the core issue underlying such limited transferability is the coupling of reasoning and knowledge in LLMs. To transition from AUI to AGI, we propose disentangling knowledge and reasoning through three key directions: (1) pretaining to reason using RL from scratch as an alternative to the widely used next-token prediction pretraining, (2) using a curriculum of synthetic tasks to ease the learning of a \textit{reasoning prior} for RL that can then be transferred to natural language tasks, and (3) learning more generalizable reasoning functions using a small context window to reduce exploiting spurious correlations between tokens. Such a reasoning system coupled with a trained retrieval system and a large external memory bank as a knowledge store can overcome several limitations of existing architectures at learning to reason in novel scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper argues that Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit limited transferability in algorithmic reasoning tasks due to the coupling of knowledge and reasoning during supervised pretraining (SPT).  The authors hypothesize that next-token prediction on Internet-scale data leads to overfitting to statistical patterns, hindering the development of robust reasoning abilities. They propose disentangling knowledge and reasoning through: 1) pretraining for reasoning via reinforcement learning (RPT) from scratch, 2) using synthetic tasks to develop a reasoning prior for RL, and 3) architectural changes (external memory and small context windows) to decouple knowledge storage from reasoning.  They present some initial experiments supporting their claim that SPT can restrict RL fine-tuning and that RPT can lead to better performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper identifies a critical weakness in the prevailing paradigm of LLM development: the reliance on passive data during pretraining, which may stifle the development of genuine, transferable reasoning. The analogy to AlphaGo/AlphaZero is compelling and effectively motivates the central argument. The identified need to disentangle knowledge and reasoning aligns with broader trends in AI towards more modular and interpretable systems. However, the paper is primarily a position paper and suffers from several shortcomings:

*   **Limited Empirical Evidence:** While the paper includes some experimental results, they are preliminary and far from conclusive. The Brainf**k/Befunge experiments illustrate the transferability issue but don't definitively prove *why* SPT is the culprit or that RPT solves it. The Go experiment, while suggestive, is a vastly simplified environment compared to LLM pretraining.  Similarly, the synthetic math task experiment shows an advantage for RFT over SFT-then-RFT, which is promising, but the scope is narrow.
*   **Lack of Concrete Implementation Details:** The proposed directions are somewhat vague. While the ideas are interesting, the paper lacks detailed specifications on how to implement RPT effectively for language, design the optimal curriculum of synthetic tasks, or learn the memory access mechanisms in the proposed architecture.  The challenge of efficient exploration for RPT is acknowledged, but solutions are not well-developed.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** The core idea of disentangling knowledge and reasoning is not entirely new. Previous work has explored modular architectures and memory-augmented neural networks. The contribution lies in applying these concepts specifically to the challenges of algorithmic reasoning in LLMs, highlighting the limitations of the current pretraining paradigm, and framing it within the AUI-to-AGI transition. The importance of iterative reasoning has also been previously explored within the Chain-of-Thought literature.
*   **Unproven Scalability:** The benefits of the proposed approach are demonstrated on small scale. The central hypothesis that the suggested framework can solve the scalability problems of previous models remains unvalidated. It is also uncertain if the benefits from the proposed framework outweigh the costs of training.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear articulation of the problem of limited transferability.
*   Compelling analogy to AlphaGo/AlphaZero.
*   Well-motivated proposal for disentangling knowledge and reasoning.
*   Identification of key challenges in the current paradigm.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited experimental validation.
*   Lack of concrete implementation details.
*   Overstated novelty.
*   Lack of discussion of potential drawbacks or limitations of the proposed approach.
*   Experiments do not thoroughly evaluate all proposed research directions

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence the field by prompting researchers to reconsider the dominant pretraining paradigm and explore alternative approaches that prioritize robust reasoning over pattern memorization. The emphasis on disentanglement could also inspire new architectures and training methods. However, the impact will depend on the feasibility and effectiveness of the proposed directions when implemented at scale.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting a critical issue in LLM reasoning and proposing potentially fruitful research directions. The AlphaGo/AlphaZero analogy is strong and the argument for disentanglement is well-reasoned. However, the lack of substantial empirical support and concrete implementation details significantly limits the paper's impact. The novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary. A higher score would require more compelling evidence and a more detailed roadmap for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Learning Code-Edit Embedding to Model Student Debugging Behavior
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19407v1)
- **Authors**: Hasnain Heickal, Andrew Lan
- **Abstract**: Providing effective feedback for programming assignments in computer science education can be challenging: students solve problems by iteratively submitting code, executing it, and using limited feedback from the compiler or the auto-grader to debug. Analyzing student debugging behavior in this process may reveal important insights into their knowledge and inform better personalized support tools. In this work, we propose an encoder-decoder-based model that learns meaningful code-edit embeddings between consecutive student code submissions, to capture their debugging behavior. Our model leverages information on whether a student code submission passes each test case to fine-tune large language models (LLMs) to learn code editing representations. It enables personalized next-step code suggestions that maintain the student's coding style while improving test case correctness. Our model also enables us to analyze student code-editing patterns to uncover common student errors and debugging behaviors, using clustering techniques. Experimental results on a real-world student code submission dataset demonstrate that our model excels at code reconstruction and personalized code suggestion while revealing interesting patterns in student debugging behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper followed by a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an encoder-decoder model that learns code-edit embeddings to model student debugging behavior in programming assignments. The model leverages information on whether a student's code submission passes test cases to fine-tune a large language model (CodeT5) to learn code-editing representations.  It uses a contrastive loss, a reconstruction loss, and a regularization loss to guide the model.  The approach allows for personalized next-step code suggestions, and also enables the analysis of student code-editing patterns. Experiments on a real-world student code submission dataset demonstrate the model's effectiveness in code reconstruction and personalized code suggestion. The authors also show how clustering these embeddings can reveal common student errors and debugging strategies. They compare their model against GPT-4o and other baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its explicit modeling of code *edits* (changes between submissions) to understand and assist student debugging. While prior work has focused on generating feedback or hints based on single submissions, this approach leverages the iterative nature of debugging. Specifically the use of code-edit embeddings to personalize next-step suggestions and uncover common errors via clustering is a notable contribution. Fine-tuning a CodeT5 model with contrastive, reconstruction, and regularization loss to capture code changes guided by test case outcomes appears novel as well. The paper moves beyond simply providing correct solutions (which GPT-4 often does) by aiming for incremental improvements and guidance.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the potential to improve personalized feedback and support in programming education. The ability to provide tailored next-step suggestions that maintain a student's coding style and target specific errors incrementally is a promising direction. Clustering code-edit embeddings to identify common debugging strategies can also help instructors improve course materials and address recurring challenges more effectively. The comparison to strong baselines like GPT-4o adds weight to the claims. However, the observed limitations, like the propensity of the method to produce code with syntactical errors need further resolving. In the future, a post processing of these errors via LLMs can effectively remedy such errors.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper tackles an important problem in programming education.
*   **Novel Approach:** Modeling code edits and using contrastive learning guided by test case outcomes is a novel approach.
*   **Experimental Validation:**  Experiments on a real-world dataset and comparison to strong baselines (including GPT-4o) support the claims.
*   **Potential for Impact:** The approach has the potential to improve personalized feedback and instructional strategies.
*   **Error pattern identification:** Clustering based on the method allows error patterns to be identified.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Syntactical Errors:** The model occasionally generates syntactically incorrect code, which limits its practicality without further improvement.  While the paper acknowledges this, it's a significant hurdle.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the dataset is real-world, the evaluation focuses primarily on CodeBLEU scores and adherence to test-case masks. More qualitative analysis of the suggestions and their impact on student learning would strengthen the paper.
*   **Dependence on Test Cases:** The reliance on test case feedback for guiding the contrastive learning limits the applicability to programming environments where detailed test cases are not available or reliable. The method might need adjustments for scenarios involving more open-ended or creative programming tasks where a ground truth test case mask cannot be defined.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach with demonstrated effectiveness in modeling code edits for personalized next-step suggestions and error analysis. The experimental results are promising, and the potential for impact in programming education is significant. However, the limitation of generating code with syntax errors prevents it from obtaining a higher score. While the contribution is solid and presents a strong base for future research, some aspects like the syntactical errors and the dependency on test cases limit the approach at the moment. Therefore, based on my rigorous evaluation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### ImageChain: Advancing Sequential Image-to-Text Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19409v1)
- **Authors**: Danae Sánchez Villegas, Ingo Ziegler, Desmond Elliott
- **Abstract**: Reasoning over sequences of images remains a challenge for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). While recent models incorporate multi-image data during pre-training, they still struggle to recognize sequential structures, often treating images independently. This work introduces ImageChain, a framework that enhances MLLMs with sequential reasoning capabilities over image data by modeling visual sequences as a multi-turn conversation. In ImageChain, images are interleaved with corresponding textual descriptions to form a controlled dialogue that explicitly captures temporal dependencies and narrative progression. Our method optimizes for the task of next-scene description, where the model generates a context-aware description of an upcoming scene based on preceding visual and textual cues. We demonstrate that our approach improves performance on the next-scene description task -- achieving an average improvement from 3.7% to 19% in SimRate, a metric that quantifies semantic similarity to human-annotated ground truths. Moreover, ImageChain achieves robust zero-shot out-of-domain performance in applications ranging from comics to robotics. Extensive experiments validate that instruction-tuning in a multimodal, multi-turn conversation design is key to bridging the gap between static image understanding and temporally-aware reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IMAGECHAIN, a framework designed to enhance the sequential reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  It addresses the limitation of existing MLLMs that often process image sequences independently, failing to capture temporal dependencies. IMAGECHAIN models image sequences as multi-turn conversations, interleaving images with corresponding textual descriptions. The framework is optimized for the task of next-scene description, where the model generates context-aware descriptions of upcoming scenes based on preceding visual and textual cues.  The authors demonstrate significant improvements in performance on next-scene description, using a metric called SimRate, and also showcase robust zero-shot out-of-domain performance in applications like comics and robotics. A repurposed video dataset, StoryFrames, is introduced to support the method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to improve sequential visual reasoning in MLLMs. The idea of framing a visual sequence as a multi-turn conversation is interesting and relatively straightforward to implement. The reported improvements on the next-scene description task, particularly the substantial increase in SimRate compared to standard fine-tuning, is a significant strength.  The zero-shot out-of-domain performance, especially the doubling of F1 score on the robotics task, lends further credence to the effectiveness of the proposed method.

However, several aspects warrant critical scrutiny:

*   **Novelty:** While framing visual sequences as conversations isn't entirely new in other areas of NLP, its application to MLLMs for *sequential* reasoning is a meaningful adaptation. The core novelty lies in the *specific* design of the conversation (image interleaved with text). However, the overall concept builds on existing works related to visual storytelling and MLLM architectures, rather than representing a fundamentally new paradigm.

*   **Significance:** The limitations of current MLLMs in capturing temporal dynamics are well-established. IMAGECHAIN offers a practical and demonstrably effective method to address this gap.  The authors' clear demonstration of improvement in multiple settings (in-domain and out-of-domain) underscores the potential practical value of the method. The creation of StoryFrames, the dataset, is valuable for promoting future research in this area.

*   **Technical Depth:**  The method itself is relatively simple, relying on standard instruction tuning and a fixed conversation structure. While simplicity can be an advantage, it also means that the technical contributions are not deeply transformative.  The core of the technique is in the *design* of the multi-turn prompts. Future work could explore more sophisticated techniques for dynamically adjusting the conversation structure.

*   **Experimental Rigor:**  The experiments appear well-conducted, including thorough baselines and comparisons.  However, the evaluation relies heavily on SimRate, which is itself assessed by another LLM (Llama 3 70B). While LLM-as-a-judge approaches are gaining traction, they are not foolproof and can introduce biases.  Furthermore, the exact prompt is mentioned to be in the appendix, this could be analyzed further. Human evaluation of a subset of results could strengthen the validation. The claims about robustness could be enhanced with experiments under more challenging noise and adversarial settings.

*   **Generalizability:** The reliance on relatively small datasets (approx. 4000 training examples) raises concerns about generalizability to drastically different visual domains or tasks. While out-of-domain performance is good, evaluating on even *more* diverse datasets and tasks would provide additional confidence.

*   **Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations regarding longer sequences and relying on simple averaging for scene-level visual representations. Future work should address these shortcomings. Also, more details about the hyperparameter tuning process would be useful.

**Justification for Score:**

IMAGECHAIN offers a valuable practical improvement to MLLMs for sequential visual reasoning. The approach is relatively simple yet demonstrably effective, and the introduction of the StoryFrames dataset is a useful contribution. However, the limited technical depth and reliance on LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, and simple approach for visual data encoding alongside other minor limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Less or More: Towards Glanceable Explanations for LLM Recommendations Using Ultra-Small Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19410v1)
- **Authors**: Xinru Wang, Mengjie Yu, Hannah Nguyen, Michael Iuzzolino, Tianyi Wang, Peiqi Tang, Natasha Lynova, Co Tran, Ting Zhang, Naveen Sendhilnathan, Hrvoje Benko, Haijun Xia, Tanya Jonker
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable potential in recommending everyday actions as personal AI assistants, while Explainable AI (XAI) techniques are being increasingly utilized to help users understand why a recommendation is given. Personal AI assistants today are often located on ultra-small devices such as smartwatches, which have limited screen space. The verbosity of LLM-generated explanations, however, makes it challenging to deliver glanceable LLM explanations on such ultra-small devices. To address this, we explored 1) spatially structuring an LLM's explanation text using defined contextual components during prompting and 2) presenting temporally adaptive explanations to users based on confidence levels. We conducted a user study to understand how these approaches impacted user experiences when interacting with LLM recommendations and explanations on ultra-small devices. The results showed that structured explanations reduced users' time to action and cognitive load when reading an explanation. Always-on structured explanations increased users' acceptance of AI recommendations. However, users were less satisfied with structured explanations compared to unstructured ones due to their lack of sufficient, readable details. Additionally, adaptively presenting structured explanations was less effective at improving user perceptions of the AI compared to the always-on structured explanations. Together with users' interview feedback, the results led to design implications to be mindful of when personalizing the content and timing of LLM explanations that are displayed on ultra-small devices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Less or More: Towards Glanceable Explanations for LLM Recommendations Using Ultra-Small Devices":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of providing explainable AI (XAI) for Large Language Model (LLM) recommendations on ultra-small devices, such as smartwatches, where screen space is severely limited.  The authors explore two approaches: 1) spatially structuring LLM explanations using defined contextual components (activity, object, location, goal) and 2) temporally adapting explanations based on recommendation confidence. They conduct a user study to evaluate the impact of these approaches on user experience.  The results indicate that structured explanations reduce task time and cognitive load and increase acceptance rates, but users find them less satisfying than unstructured explanations. Adaptive explanations, based on confidence levels, were less effective than always-on structured explanations. The paper concludes with design implications for optimizing the content and timing of glanceable LLM explanations on ultra-small devices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution lies in addressing a very specific, and increasingly relevant, problem: XAI on ultra-small devices *specifically in the context of LLM-based recommendations*. While XAI and UI design for small screens are individually established areas, the intersection of these with the emergence of LLMs creates a unique challenge.  The application of structured prompting to generate spatially glanceable explanations is moderately novel. The hybrid method for confidence estimation and adaptive presentation draws from existing work but its specific implementation in this context adds incremental novelty. There is a lack of exploration in alternative visual methods of presentation such as using other forms of diagrams or graphs to explain the reasoning, however the paper focuses on LLM methods of explanation.

*   **Significance:** The work holds practical significance. As personal AI assistants become more prevalent on wearable devices, the need for clear and concise explanations becomes critical for user trust, acceptance, and effective human-AI collaboration. The empirical evaluation provides valuable insights into the trade-offs between different XAI strategies for ultra-small devices, informing design decisions. The conclusion offers valuable guidance for designers and researchers in this area. The limited generalizability and the lack of real-world implementation reduces the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and its relevance.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The user study is well-designed, with a controlled experiment and both quantitative and qualitative data collection.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The design implications are concrete and actionable.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The statistical analysis done is very comprehensive in terms of identifying possible variables of interest and possible relationships between them.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The study is based on simulated scenarios and may not fully reflect real-world user behavior. The Ego4D dataset videos, while diverse, represent specific contexts, limiting generalization to other types of AI assistance or environments. The experiment does not account for the novelty effect, in which participants may act differently simply due to the novelty of the experiment.
    *   **User Feedback:** The usability results that describe user's desire for more transparent AI are at odds with the results that point to reduced cognitive load from the structured explanations. More work needs to be done to address this problem.
    *   **Lack of benchmark datasets:** It would be interesting to compare the results of this pipeline with commonly used benchmark explanation datasets.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Modalities:** The study primarily focuses on textual explanations with icons. Exploring alternative modalities (e.g., audio cues, haptic feedback) would expand the scope of the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of XAI systems for ultra-small devices, prompting further research into more effective methods for delivering glanceable and trustworthy explanations. It can also serve as a foundation for developing theoretical frameworks that guide XAI design in constrained environments.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to a specific and emerging area. However, the limitations in generalizability, and the lack of comparison benchmarks temper the overall impact. The paper tackles a real and growing issue with LLM recommendations in the context of personal AI assistants. While it builds upon existing research in XAI and UI design, the combination of these elements in the context of ultra-small devices gives it sufficient novelty to warrant a higher-than-average score. The careful study and extraction of design implications strengthens the significance of the work.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Code to Think, Think to Code: A Survey on Code-Enhanced Reasoning and Reasoning-Driven Code Intelligence in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19411v1)
- **Authors**: Dayu Yang, Tianyang Liu, Daoan Zhang, Antoine Simoulin, Xiaoyi Liu, Yuwei Cao, Zhaopu Teng, Xin Qian, Grey Yang, Jiebo Luo, Julian McAuley
- **Abstract**: In large language models (LLMs), code and reasoning reinforce each other: code offers an abstract, modular, and logic-driven structure that supports reasoning, while reasoning translates high-level goals into smaller, executable steps that drive more advanced code intelligence. In this study, we examine how code serves as a structured medium for enhancing reasoning: it provides verifiable execution paths, enforces logical decomposition, and enables runtime validation. We also explore how improvements in reasoning have transformed code intelligence from basic completion to advanced capabilities, enabling models to address complex software engineering tasks through planning and debugging. Finally, we identify key challenges and propose future research directions to strengthen this synergy, ultimately improving LLM's performance in both areas.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "Code to Think, Think to Code: A Survey on Code-Enhanced Reasoning and Reasoning-Driven Code Intelligence in LLMs."

**Summary:**

The paper surveys the interplay between code and reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). It explores how code can enhance reasoning capabilities by providing a structured medium with verifiable execution paths, logical decomposition, and runtime validation. Conversely, it examines how advances in LLM reasoning have transformed code intelligence, enabling models to perform complex software engineering tasks such as planning and debugging.  The paper categorizes different approaches, including code-aided reasoning, training with code, reasoning for code generation, reasoning over code, and interactive programming. It highlights the challenges and proposes future research directions, focusing on interpretability, scalability, and multimodal fusion.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's core novelty lies in providing a comprehensive survey that consolidates and synthesizes a wide range of recent research at the intersection of code and reasoning in LLMs.  While the individual concepts and methods discussed are not novel in themselves (they are referenced works), the *systematic mapping* of these concepts into a cohesive framework *is* a valuable contribution.  Previous works have often focused on specific aspects of code generation or reasoning independently.  This survey bridges that gap.

However, the survey could be more critical in its evaluation of existing work. While it acknowledges challenges, it often presents a relatively positive picture, without deeply dissecting the limitations of various approaches. The evaluation of the work was based solely on what the original paper states, with no evaluation conducted separately to find contradictory results. A survey paper has to have an in-depth knowledge of the different approaches being presented.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its ability to provide a clear roadmap for researchers and practitioners interested in this rapidly evolving field. By identifying key challenges and proposing future research directions, it helps to focus efforts on the most promising areas.  Specifically, the emphasis on interpretability, scalability, multimodal fusion, and addressing limitations in long-context understanding are all highly relevant.

The organization of the paper into categories such as "Code-enhanced Reasoning" and "Reasoning-enhanced Code Intelligence" is useful for understanding the bidirectional relationship between code and reasoning. The taxonomy presented in Figure 2 and Table 3, showing the interconnection between different approaches and the specific LLMs used with these approaches, is highly beneficial.

**Weaknesses:**

1.  **Limited Critical Analysis:** As mentioned previously, the survey would benefit from a more critical analysis of the limitations of existing approaches.  For example, a deeper discussion of the potential pitfalls of relying too heavily on code generation for reasoning, or the challenges of scaling reasoning-enhanced code intelligence to real-world software engineering projects, would enhance the paper's value.
2.  **Bias Toward Success Stories:** Surveys are susceptible to cherry-picking successes. While the paper attempts a comprehensive overview, it doesn't provide a nuanced view of the failures or shortcomings of different models and approaches. The inclusion of such information would provide a more balanced assessment of the field's current state.
3.  **Somewhat Descriptive, Less Prescriptive:** While it offers some future research directions, the paper could be stronger in prescribing specific concrete research agendas. For example, instead of broadly suggesting "improving interpretability," it could delve into specific techniques (e.g., formal verification, attention analysis) that might be particularly effective.
4.  **Missing External Validation:** The survey does not include a new framework or model, and therefore can not evaluate the impact with it's own experiments.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is valuable as a comprehensive and well-organized summary of recent research in a fast-moving field. It provides a useful framework for understanding the interaction between code and reasoning in LLMs. However, its lack of critical analysis, its tendency to focus on positive results, and its limited prescriptive guidance prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Norm Growth and Stability Challenges in Localized Sequential Knowledge Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19416v1)
- **Authors**: Akshat Gupta, Christine Fang, Atahan Ozdemir, Maochuan Lu, Ahmed Alaa, Thomas Hartvigsen, Gopala Anumanchipalli
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the impact of localized updates to large language models (LLMs), specifically in the context of knowledge editing - a task aimed at incorporating or modifying specific facts without altering broader model capabilities. We first show that across different post-training interventions like continuous pre-training, full fine-tuning and LORA-based fine-tuning, the Frobenius norm of the updated matrices always increases. This increasing norm is especially detrimental for localized knowledge editing, where only a subset of matrices are updated in a model . We reveal a consistent phenomenon across various editing techniques, including fine-tuning, hypernetwork-based approaches, and locate-and-edit methods: the norm of the updated matrix invariably increases with successive updates. Such growth disrupts model balance, particularly when isolated matrices are updated while the rest of the model remains static, leading to potential instability and degradation of downstream performance. Upon deeper investigations of the intermediate activation vectors, we find that the norm of internal activations decreases and is accompanied by shifts in the subspaces occupied by these activations, which shows that these activation vectors now occupy completely different regions in the representation space compared to the unedited model. With our paper, we highlight the technical challenges with continuous and localized sequential knowledge editing and their implications for maintaining model stability and utility.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the phenomenon of norm growth in weight matrices during various post-training interventions, including knowledge editing. It observes that the Frobenius norm of updated matrices consistently increases across different techniques like continual pre-training, fine-tuning (full and LORA-based), and knowledge editing methods (ROME, MEMIT, MEND, PMET). The paper argues that this norm growth, especially when localized in knowledge editing, disrupts model balance, leading to instability and degraded performance. It further analyzes the impact on hidden activations, finding a decrease in norm and shifts in the subspaces they occupy, suggesting changes in the model's representation space. The paper highlights the challenges of continuous and localized sequential knowledge editing for maintaining model stability and utility. A follow-up work (Gupta et al., 2025) is mentioned, claiming to address these limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper tackles an important practical problem in the emerging field of knowledge editing and incremental learning of large language models. The core observation – the consistent increase in the norm of updated weight matrices during post-training interventions – is valuable and empirically well-supported.

**Strengths:**

*   **Empirical Breadth:** The study comprehensively examines a wide range of post-training interventions (CPT, FFT, LORA) and knowledge editing methods (ROME, MEMIT, MEND, PMET) across different model sizes (GPT2-XL, GPT-J, Llama2-7B, Starcoder). This breadth enhances the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of norm growth in the context of localized knowledge editing and its potential impact on model stability. This provides focus for the analysis.
*   **Insightful Analysis of Activations:** The investigation of hidden activations provides further insight into how the norm growth affects the internal representations of the model. The change in norm and the shifted activation spaces contribute to a more complete picture of the phenomenon.
*   **Motivates Future Work:** The paper appropriately frames the problem and motivates future research by suggesting the need for innovative strategies to address the challenges of norm growth. It also references Gupta et al. (2025) which supposedly addresses the limitations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Theoretical Explanation:** While the paper presents compelling empirical evidence, it lacks a strong theoretical explanation for why norm growth consistently occurs.  The triangle inequality argument (Eq. 2) only shows the *possibility* of increase, but doesn't explain the *tendency*.  A more in-depth discussion of gradient descent dynamics and optimization landscapes would strengthen the paper.
*   **Limited Countermeasures:** The paper mentions a follow-up study that supposedly addresses the norm growth issue, but provides very little detail, making it difficult to assess the true practical significance.
*   **Downstream Performance Degradation:** The downstream performance degradation is shown, but is more an observation of something bad happening. It is less a contribution to the norm growth discussion. More analysis of the specific cause of the downsteam performance degradations would have strengthened the paper.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the systematic empirical documentation of norm growth across a diverse set of methods and models, specifically connecting it to the challenges of localized knowledge editing. The significance is in highlighting a practical problem that can hinder the development of stable and scalable knowledge editing systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable empirical contribution by documenting a consistent trend of norm growth and linking it to potential problems in knowledge editing. However, the lack of a strong theoretical explanation and very little detail on the (supposedly) related follow-up study detract from its overall impact. The downstream results are helpful but limited. It serves as a strong warning flag, but is primarily empirical.

Score: 6

The paper is good but it lacks theoretical grounding.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10
